# APPENDIX D — Round-2 skeptic panels and critic; Round-3 adjudications, reads and critic
# Round-2 adversarial skeptic panels (verbatim structured returns)

## Pawlak et al. 2026, bioRxiv 10.64898/2026.05.04.722774 — Mapping California's Urban Forest at Scale

### Lens: archive-and-epochs (C1, C2) — overall 3, clears_bar=False
SUMMARY: On the archive-and-epochs lens the candidate loses the one criterion it claimed. C1 is refuted: the imagery is aerial and sub-meter, and the text does say the imagery is 'collected and processed by multiple contractors' — but the same sentence says it is 'assembled into a single statewide product', so the archive is ONE delivery program (NAIP) at a constant 60 cm, 4-band RGB+NIR, not a multi-program heterogeneous archive; and 'without radiometric calibration' is never stated in the paper, whose only named cross-year hazards are geometric (parallax, off-nadir, orthorectification, sun elevation). A Y there rests on outside knowledge of NAIP, not on the page — score '?'/N, refuted. C2 is uncontested: 'a four-year (2016, 2018, 2020, 2022) 60-cm canopy cover time series' — 4 epochs, 6-year span, failing both the ≥5-epoch and ≥10-year thresholds. Carrying the reader's out-of-lens Y scores for C3, C4 and C5 unchanged gives 3/5, below the 4/5 bar. Note also that the paper's own limitation section forbids the pixel-to-pixel comparison the target problem needs ('canopy products produced from NAIP imagery should not be used for pixel-to-pixel change comparisons'), which further weakens the case that this is a twin of a heterogeneous multi-vendor archive problem.
- C1 — aerial (not satellite) sub-meter imagery from a MULTI-vendor/multi-program archive without radiometric calibration: claimed=Y | skeptic=? | refuted=True | page=p.6 (lines 125-126) and p.26 (lines 528-531) | quote=p.6: "U-Net inputs were NAIP multispectral imagery, collected approximately every two years. In California, NAIP imagery has been available at 60 cm resolution since 2016." | p.26: "NAIP mosaics vary in orthorectification quality and off-nadir viewing angle both spatially across California and temporally across acquisition years because imagery is collected and processed by multiple contractors and then assembled into a single statewide product." | reason=Three of the sub-elements split. PASS: aerial (NAIP is airborne, not satellite) and sub-meter (60 cm, uniform across all four years). PASS-ish: multi-contractor is stated in text ('collected and processed by multiple contractors'), which is the strongest support for the 'multi-vendor' half. FAIL: 'multi-program' is contradicted by the same sentence — the contractors' work is 'assembled into a single statewide product', i.e. ONE federal delivery program (NAIP) with one uniform specification per year, 4-band RGB+NIR, constant 60 cm GSD. That is the opposite of the target archive's 5+ delivery programs, 5 cm-1 m GSD spread, mostly-RGB heterogeneity; the LiDAR from 3DEP is training-label input, not part of the mapped time series. FAIL: 'without radiometric calibration' is never stated anywhere in the paper. Every cross-year hazard the authors name is geometric — parallax, off-nadir angle, orthorectification quality, sun-elevation shadows (pp.26-27) — and radiometry is not discussed at all. Scoring Y on that clause requires outside knowledge of how NAIP is produced, which is precisely the inference-not-text this lens is instructed to refute. With the multi-program half false on the page and the radiometric half unstated, the Y does not survive; '?' at best, and by the default-refute rule the claimed Y is refuted.
- C2 — ≥5 epochs spanning ≥10 years: claimed=N | skeptic=N | refuted=False | page=p.4 (lines 76-77); corroborated p.5 (lines 110-112) and p.22 (line 430) | quote="To track urban canopy change in California, we produced a four-year (2016, 2018, 2020, 2022) 60-cm canopy cover time series for all California census-designated places (CDPs)" | reason=Counted exactly as printed: 4 epochs (2016, 2018, 2020, 2022), span 2016-2022 = 6 years. Fails both thresholds (≥5 epochs, ≥10 years), and by a wide margin on span. Confirmed independently by the methods ('applied to the CDPs within each climate zone across California using imagery from 2016, 2018, 2020, and 2022') and the discussion ('across four acquisition years at 60 cm resolution'). No fifth epoch or earlier imagery appears anywhere, including Appendices C and D; the 2016-2021 3DEP LiDAR datasets in Appendix C are training inputs, not time-series epochs. The reader's N stands — nothing to refute.

### Lens: method-and-metric (C3, C4) — overall 3, clears_bar=False
SUMMARY: Within the method-and-metric lens, C3 survives cleanly and C4 does not. Pawlak et al. 2026 is unambiguously a wall-to-wall automated segmentation product (U-Net per-pixel probability thresholded at 0.65, 60 cm, 39,032 km2, four years, rasters public), so C3 = Y as claimed. C4 = N, refuted. The reader's first prong (per-year PA .64-.82) is per-date accuracy against each year's own reference labels -- the one evidence type this lens rules out by name -- and the paper never converts it into an inter-date quantity. The second prong (Fig. 8) is 11 hand-picked, manually digitized crowns chosen because artifacts were 'visually apparent', with no number in the body text and an explicit authorial disclaimer that it 'is not intended to quantify statewide bias' and that estimating 'how frequently these distortions occur' is future work. The paper further declines pixel-to-pixel comparison entirely ('we did not analyze small scale ... change'), so no flicker, spurious-change, or inter-date agreement statistic exists. The obvious sawtooth in its own interval estimates (-6.4/-6.6/-8.6 pp in 2018-2020, +7.8 pp rebound) is attributed to 'a combination of real canopy loss and view angle acquisition variability' without partitioning -- acknowledged, not quantified. This paper is a strong Olofsson-style area-estimation twin and a weak temporal-consistency twin; it measures uncertainty in per-year area, not inconsistency between dates. Carrying the reader's out-of-lens C1=Y, C2=N, C5=Y unchanged, the twin scores 3/5 and does not clear the bar.
- C3: claimed=Y | skeptic=Y | refuted=False | page=p. 4 and p. 9 | quote=we produced a four-year (2016, 2018, 2020, 2022) 60-cm canopy cover time series for all California census-designated places (CDPs) ... the final 1 x 1 convolution produced a per-pixel canopy probability map. These probabilities were converted to a binary output by applying a threshold of 0.65 | reason=Unrefutable. This is a genuine wall-to-wall automated segmentation product, not a dot grid: a U-Net (ResNet-34 encoder) emitting per-pixel canopy probability, thresholded to a binary raster, applied across 39,032 km2 of all California CDPs for four acquisition years, with rasters and checkpoints published on Dryad. The 1,700-point sample exists only as the accuracy/area-adjustment reference, not as the canopy product. C3 stands as claimed.
- C4: claimed=Y (weak: per-year PA .64-.82 under a fixed model; 11-crown parallax experiment Fig. 8) | skeptic=N | refuted=True | page=p. 27 | quote=While this experiment is not intended to quantify statewide bias, it demonstrates that acquisition geometry and orthorectification can generate canopy change signals at the small scale that are not related to tree growth. ... Future work should estimate how frequently these distortions occur and evaluate their contribution to uncertainty in statewide canopy cover and change estimates. | reason=Both prongs of the claimed Y fail. (1) Per-year PA .64/.66/.82/.69 (Table 3, p. 17) is per-date accuracy of each year's map against that same year's own reference labels -- exactly the evidence this lens bars. A PA swing is a per-epoch quality statistic; it is not flicker, spurious change, inter-date agreement, or a detectable-change threshold, and the paper never converts it into one. (2) The Fig. 8 parallax exercise is a demonstration, not a metric: n=11 crowns, manually digitized, in 'locations where view-angle artifacts were hand selected to be visually apparent' (p. 26) -- deliberately non-representative; no numeric value appears anywhere in the body text (only the figure's own 'interval means', which the caption disclaims: 'Interval means are shown only to indicate the magnitude of variability and should not be interpreted as typical change'); and the authors themselves state it is 'not intended to quantify statewide bias' and consign the quantification to future work. The authors also explicitly declined to compute any inter-date pixel agreement at all: 'canopy products produced from NAIP imagery should not be used for pixel-to-pixel change comparisons. For that reason, we did not analyze small scale (on each parcel, or by tree) change' (p. 26). The strongest counter -- the sawtooth visible in the interval Wald-Z results (-6.38%, -6.58%, -8.60% in 2018-2020, then +7.80% rebound in the Inland Empire) -- is left unpartitioned: 'This canopy decrease may reflect a combination of real canopy loss and view angle acquisition variability' (p. 22). That is temporal inconsistency acknowledged, with no number separating artifact from signal, which is precisely the case C4 excludes. Verdict: N.

### Lens: validation-design (C5) — overall 3, clears_bar=False
SUMMARY: C5 refuted; Pawlak et al. 2026 drops to 3/5 and no longer clears the twin bar. The paper does collect paired reference data — the same ~1,700-point sample is re-interpreted against each year's NAIP imagery, and the per-year reference labels genuinely shift — so it is not stable-points-only and the reader's Y is defensible about the data collection. But the estimator never uses the pairing: there is no transition matrix, no count of changed points, no paired/covariance variance term, and no reference change quantity is ever compared to a mapped change quantity. Worse, the sample was allocated once on the 2020 map (600/600 mapped canopy/non-canopy, 2020 urban strata) while the Olofsson estimator re-stratifies on each year's own raster with equal within-stratum weights — so the inclusion probabilities are wrong for 2016, 2018 and 2022, and the predicted artifact (a 2020-centered dip) is exactly what Table 3 shows: 24.89 / 24.16 / 18.32 / 21.58 pp, with reference canopy points PEAKING in 2020 (47.9%) in the year the adjusted estimate bottoms out. The estimate tracks the per-date operating point W_h, not the reference. The headline Sen's slope CI is percentiles of six pairwise slopes from four epochs and never ingests the per-year SEs, so the trend is produced without validated uncertainty. The paper concedes it cannot do pixel-to-pixel change and did not attempt parcel- or tree-level change; Fig. 8 (11 hand-picked crowns, explicitly not a bias estimate) is a noise demonstration, not a change validation. Reader scores outside the lens (C1 Y, C2 N, C3 Y, C4 Y-weak) left standing, though C4's Y rests on the same thin Fig. 8 exercise plus a per-date PA spread and deserves its own scrutiny.
- C5 — change/trend estimate produced WITH a validation design that can see change: claimed=Y (per-year re-interpreted 1,700 points, Olofsson-adjusted areas, Wald Z, Sen slope) | skeptic=N | refuted=True | page=pp. 11-12 (Methods 2.8), with pp. 13-14 (2.9) and p. 27 (4.5) | quote="Initially, 1,200 points were randomly placed within 2020 urban areas (600 on mapped canopy, 600 on mapped non-canopy). Then 500 points were placed in non-urban areas with a 50/50 split for mapped canopy and non-canopy... Each point was validated once per year against NAIP imagery (4,800 unique validations were completed in this process)." (p.11, l.239-245) ... "For each year, we defined two map strata based on the classified raster: canopy (mapped class 1) and non-canopy (mapped class 0)." (p.12, l.255-257) ... "where nhj is the count of reference samples in class j within the mapped stratum h, and nh is the total number of reference points in that stratum." (p.13, l.267-268) | reason=CONCEDED: the paired re-reads are real. The same sample is re-interpreted against each year's imagery ("validated once per year"), and the per-year reference counts genuinely move (canopy ref points 637/672/823/627; Table 3, p.17), so this is not a stable-points-only design and the reader is right about the data COLLECTION. The refutation is that the ESTIMATOR discards that pairing and cannot see change. (1) Strata mismatch, stated not inferred: the sample was allocated ONCE on the 2020 map (600/600 mapped canopy/non-canopy; geographic strata explicitly "urban and non-urban (in 2020)", l.227-228), yet the estimator re-stratifies by "that year's" classified raster and takes equal within-stratum weights n_hj/n_h. Inclusion probabilities were set by 2020 stratum areas (~4:1 non-canopy:canopy), so inside a 2016/2018/2022 stratum the 2020-canopy points are grossly overweighted; no Stehman-type correction is mentioned. Equal weights are correct only for 2020 — the one year the estimate dips. The predicted artifact is exactly Table 3: 24.89 -> 24.16 -> 18.32 -> 21.58, i.e. the F1 sawtooth, and the reference share of canopy PEAKS in 2020 (823/1719 = 47.9%) in the very year the adjusted estimate bottoms out. The estimate is being driven by W_h, the per-date map operating point (F3), not by what the re-read points reported. (2) No paired change quantity is ever formed: no transition/change error matrix, no count of points that changed, no paired or covariance variance term. The Wald Z is defined for independent domains ("for example, urban minus non-urban within a year", p.14 l.290) and then applied across adjacent years on the SAME points with no correlation term. (3) The headline trend's uncertainty comes from no validation at all: "Uncertainty bounds for Sen's slope were approximated using the empirical distribution of pairwise slopes (2.5th-97.5th percentiles)" (p.14, l.294-295) — four epochs give six pairwise slopes, so the CI is effectively min/max of six numbers and the per-year standard errors never enter. The trend is produced, but not WITH validation. (4) The paper itself forecloses change validation: "canopy products produced from NAIP imagery should not be used for pixel-to-pixel change comparisons. For that reason, we did not analyze small scale (on each parcel, or by tree) change" (p.27, l.545-548); the Fig. 8 exercise is 11 hand-picked crowns and is explicitly "not intended to quantify statewide bias" (l.553-554), so it is a noise demonstration, not a change reference. Net: the change/trend rests on differencing per-date, per-date-adjusted map areas — the exact F1 failure mode — with the per-date correction itself mis-weighted for three of four years. This is the discriminating criterion: with C5=Y the candidate is 4/5 and clears; with C5=N it is 3/5 and does not.

## Roman et al. 2021, Land 10(4):403 — Linking Urban Tree Cover Change and Local History in a Post-Industrial City

### Lens: archive-and-epochs (C1, C2) — overall 3, clears_bar=False
SUMMARY: Within my lens the candidate splits. C2 survives cleanly and cannot be refuted: five epochs (1970/1980/1990/2000/2010) over a stated 40 years, printed both in prose on p. 5 and as five rows of Table 1 on p. 6. C1 does not survive. Only two image SOURCES appear anywhere in the paper, and both are repositories rather than vendors or acquisition programs (PASDA is a clearinghouse, DVRPC a planning commission), with four of five epochs from the same one per the p. 25 acknowledgment; no vendor, program, sensor, or flight date is ever named, so "multi-vendor/multi-program" is a reader's inference about how decadal regional aerials must have been flown, not text. And "without radiometric calibration" is never stated and is vacuous in this study: four of five epochs are grayscale film read by a single human interpreter, so no multispectral radiometry exists to cross-calibrate and the miscalibration failure mode C1 targets cannot arise. What the text DOES print — aerial, sub-meter (61/61/61/46/9.7 cm, three approximate), grayscale-to-color, 1970 partial leaf-on, and 2000/2010 as "the only years that were orthorectified" — is genuine heterogeneity incompatible with one calibrated chain, which is why I score C1 "?" rather than "N"; but an inferred multi-source, inferred-uncalibrated archive is not a Y under a lens that refuses inference. Arithmetic: Y = C2 + C4 + C5 = 3, with C3 conceded N by the reader. The bar fails on the C1 downgrade combined with C3 = N, independent of how C4 and C5 are resolved. (Aside, not scored in my lens and not changing the count: C4's 3.2 pp threshold is derived from second-INTERPRETER agreement, i.e. operator variance, not inter-DATE map consistency — defensible under "detectable-change metric" as the criterion is worded, but it is not a flicker or spurious-change census.)
- C1: claimed=Y | skeptic=? (aerial + sub-meter printed; "multi-vendor/multi-program" and "without radiometric calibration" are inference, never text) | refuted=True | page=p. 6 (Table 1 caption + footnote); p. 6 (orthorectification); p. 25 (Acknowledgments) | quote=Table 1. Aerial photos used to assess land cover. Images were obtained from the Pennsylvania Spatial Data Clearinghouse (PASDA) and the Delaware Valley Regional Planning Commission (DVRPC). ... 1 Pixel sizes for older aerials are approximate. | reason=Two of the four conjuncts in C1 are printed; two are not. PRINTED: the imagery is aerial ("Aerial photos used to assess land cover") and sub-meter at every epoch (61, 61, 61, 46, 9.7 cm), though the footnote concedes three of the five figures are approximate scan resolutions. NOT PRINTED: (a) multi-vendor/multi-program. Only two SOURCES appear anywhere in the paper, and both are repositories, not vendors or acquisition programs — PASDA is a state spatial-data clearinghouse and DVRPC is a regional planning commission — and four of the five epochs (1970, 1980, 1990, 2000) come from the same one, reinforced on p. 25: "We thank G McNichols from the Delaware Valley Regional Planning Commission for providing aerial imagery." No vendor, acquisition program, flight contractor, sensor, or flight date is named anywhere in the article. Reading DVRPC+PASDA as the analogue of the problem's "5+ delivery programs" is a reader's inference about how decadal regional aerials were flown, not something the text asserts. (b) "without radiometric calibration" is never stated — this is an argument from silence, and worse, it is vacuous here: four of the five epochs are GRAYSCALE film interpreted by a single human eye, so there is no multispectral radiometry in the archive to cross-calibrate and no radiometric pathway by which miscalibration could corrupt a detector. That is precisely the failure mode C1 exists to capture, and this archive cannot exhibit it. I stop at "?" rather than "N" only because the heterogeneity that IS printed (grayscale to color, 61 cm to 9.7 cm, 1970 "Partially on" leaf against four leaf-off dates, and "Our percent uninterpretable in 2000 (1.4%) and 2010 (0.1%)—the only years that were orthorectified") is flatly incompatible with a single calibrated processing chain. But an inferred multi-source, inferred-uncalibrated archive is not a Y under a lens that refuses inference.
- C2: claimed=Y | skeptic=Y | refuted=False | page=p. 5 (prose); Table 1, p. 6 | quote=Our analysis spanned 40 years, with imagery from 1970, 1980, 1990, 2000, and 2010 (Table 1). | reason=Unrefutable as printed. Exactly five epochs are enumerated in prose on p. 5 and independently tabulated as five rows in Table 1 on p. 6, and the span is stated as 40 years — meeting both halves of C2 (>=5 epochs, >=10 years) with margin, and immune to the OCR column-ordering ambiguity in Table 1 because the dates also appear in running text. Two caveats that do not touch the count: the cadence is decadal (five samples over 40 years, versus the problem's 37 acquisitions in 20 years, so nothing shorter than a decade is resolvable), and 1970 is "Partially on" leaf against four leaf-off dates — an uncontrolled phenology break sitting on the study's own baseline year, which the paper never quantifies. Neither is grounds to refute C2 as written.

### Lens: method-and-metric (C3, C4) — overall 4, clears_bar=True
SUMMARY: Lens verdict: no refutation. C3 is N as claimed and unarguably so — the product is a 10,000-point (8,691 usable) dot-grid photointerpretation by a single interpreter, with no polygons or raster anywhere; the paper itself names wall-to-wall coverage as the lidar property it did not have. C4 survives as Y, but as a weak Y that a reader should not over-credit. The 3.2 pp detectable-change threshold is a real, numeric, operationally-applied change floor (it demotes citywide developed-land +3.0 pp to non-detectable and drives the bolding in Tables 3-4), so it is more than per-date accuracy alone. However it is derived purely from two-interpreter disagreement summed across the endpoint dates: it quantifies human variability, not archive inconsistency. The paper never quantifies the leaf-on(1970)/leaf-off(1980-2010) discontinuity flagged in its own Table 1 — the word 'leaf' appears nowhere else in the text — never quantifies the effect of 61 cm grayscale vs 9.7 cm color, never quantifies misregistration on the three unorthorectified dates, and never counts flicker despite holding five-epoch labels per point. For the Edmonds problem this twin is evidence for F8 (human paired-point interpretation as the trend authority) and offers nothing on F1-F7; its 'temporal inconsistency' number is an inter-rater bar, not a spurious-change or processing-chain measure. Carrying the reader's C1/C2/C5 unchanged (nothing in the method sections contradicts them), Y count = 4, clears the bar — with the caveat that the two criteria it clears in my lens are C4 on a technicality of wording and not C3 at all.
- C3: claimed=N (10,000 random points) | skeptic=N | refuted=False | page=5 (also 5, 'Approach and Data Sets') | quote=We used 10,000 random points. This high number enabled us to conduct discrete analyses of different geographic units within the city (Figure 1). ... Although modern techniques, such as LiDAR, provide high resolution data with wall-to-wall coverage of a city, such datasets for UTC only emerged in the past ~15 years | reason=Confirmed N, nothing to refute — the claimed score is already N and the text is unambiguous. The canopy product is a dot-grid photointerpretation sample: 10,000 random points, 8,691 surviving after dropping 532 permanent-water and 777 uninterpretable points (p.6), each assigned one of five Nowak-and-Greenfield cover classes by a single interpreter (p.6). There are no polygons, no raster, no delineated crowns anywhere in the paper; the only wall-to-wall product mentioned is the 2008 lidar land cover, which is somebody else's dataset used as a cross-check (p.7), and the paper explicitly names wall-to-wall coverage as the property of the technique it declined to use. The two aerial figures (Figs. 4, 5) show 'Red dots ... that were interpreted for land cover' (pp.17, 21) — the map is literally dots. Consequence for the twin: a point sample can produce a citywide fraction and a paired-point trend, but it cannot exhibit or diagnose the per-pixel, edge-concentrated failure modes in F5/F6/F7 (flicker census, crown-edge dispersion, IoU between two deliveries of one flight) because there is no spatially continuous surface to be inconsistent.
- C4: claimed=Y (second-interpreter 91.5 % agreement → 3.2 pp detectable-change threshold) | skeptic=Y | refuted=False | page=7-8 (Section 2.2.2, 'Detecting UTC change') | quote=When we compared a random 5% subsample of points to a second, independent interpreter, we found 91.5% agreement regarding whether a given point was a tree across all years, with better agreement in 2000 and 2010 imagery, 92.9% and 94.9%, respectively ... The two interpreters' UTC estimates were close: the average difference across all years was 1.3 percentage points. Considering inherent user variation, and to be cautious in our conclusions, we propose a detectable change threshold ... any 40-year UTC changes larger than 3.2 percentage points are considered detectable change. | reason=I tried to refute this and could not — C4 as written enumerates 'detectable-change metric with a number' as qualifying, and p.8 states one verbatim (3.2 pp for tree; 4.7 herbaceous, 1.2 building, 9.1 other impervious). It survives the 'per-date accuracy alone' test because the number is applied operationally to reject findings, not just reported: citywide developed land is 'statistically significant but not detectable change' at +3.0 pp (p.8), and Table 4 marks districts detectable-but-not-significant and significant-but-not-detectable. Per-year agreement is also broken out (91.5 % pooled vs 92.9 %/94.9 % for 2000/2010, i.e. the older grayscale dates are demonstrably worse), which is an inter-date consistency signal with numbers. BUT this is a weak Y and the skeptic's real finding is what the 3.2 pp does NOT cover. It is an inter-rater floor, not an archive/processing-chain floor: it is built by summing two per-date human-vs-human discrepancies, so it is structurally blind to imagery-driven cross-date bias. Specifically — (1) Table 1 lists 1970 leaf status as 'Partially on' and 1980/1990/2000/2010 as 'Off'; a grep of the full text shows the word 'leaf' occurs nowhere outside Table 1's header, so the single largest cross-date confound in this archive is never discussed, let alone quantified, and a leaf-on 1970 baseline biases the +4.3 pp trend in the same direction as the reported result. (2) Only 2000 and 2010 were orthorectified (p.6); parallax and misregistration are handled by an interpreter protocol borrowed from Nowak & Greenfield with no number attached. (3) Imagery goes 61 cm grayscale to 9.7 cm color across the series, and the only per-year number tied to that heterogeneity is the uninterpretable-point rate (2.7/6.0/2.7/1.4/0.1 %), which is a data-loss rate, not a bias or consistency measure. (4) They hold five-epoch classifications for every point and could trivially have counted multi-transition (flicker) points, but never do; '65.0 % stable tree' (p.8) is presented as ecological persistence, not as an error census. So C4 is satisfied only at the level of F8 — human paired-point interpretation uncertainty — and speaks to none of F1-F7.

### Lens: validation-design (C5): is a change/trend estimate produced, and is it validated with a design that can SEE change (paired points, change-stratified sample, independent change reference) rather than stable-points-only, per-date accuracy, or map-to-map comparison? — overall 4, clears_bar=True
SUMMARY: Attacked C5 and failed to refute it: Roman et al. 2021 is a true paired-point design — the same 8,691 points interpreted at all five epochs (1970/80/90/2000/2010), transitions tabulated per point in Sankey diagrams, and change tested with matched-pairs McNemar. The second-interpreter exercise is a RANDOM 5% subsample assessed across all years (not stable-points-only), and its product is a change-level quantity: eqn. 1 combines two interpreters' 1970 and 2010 percentages into a 3.2 pp 40-year detectable-change threshold that gates every reported result ('No district had detectable levels of UTC loss'). That is not per-date accuracy and not a map-to-map comparison, so the C5 Y stands. I DO refute one leg of the claimed support: the '2008 lidar cross-check' (93.4% agreement) is a single-epoch state comparison against another map product with a two-year offset and validates no change quantity — it should be struck from the C5 rationale, which survives on the paired points plus eqn. 1 alone. Two honest weaknesses the reader should carry forward, neither sufficient to flip the score: (1) the paired points ARE the estimate, so there is no change reference independent of the estimate — this paper is the analogue of our F8 authority, not a validation of a map; (2) the 91.5% figure is interpreter PRECISION, not accuracy, and cannot detect a bias both interpreters share — and the archive invites one (1970 grayscale 61 cm 'Partially on' leaf status vs 2010 color 9.7 cm; only 2000 and 2010 orthorectified). The paper never tests whether its headline +4.3 pp is partly a leaf-status/color/resolution artifact, which is precisely our F4 failure mode. Outside my lens I contradict nothing: C1 Y (two delivery programs, sub-meter, mixed grayscale/color, no radiometric normalization described), C2 Y (5 epochs / 40 yr), C3 N (10,000 random points, no wall-to-wall product), C4 Y on the criterion's literal wording (3.2 pp detectable-change number, plus 65.0%/51.9%/89.3% persistence figures) though the quantified inconsistency is interpreter variability rather than sensor/processing inconsistency. Y count 4/5 — clears the bar, unchanged from the reader's tally, but the twin's value is as a C5/F8 methodological precedent (paired-point interpretation as trend authority, with an interpreter-derived detectable-change floor), not as evidence that cross-date radiometric bias can be ignored.
- C5 — change/trend estimate with a change-capable validation design: claimed=Y (paired points, McNemar, 2008 lidar cross-check) | skeptic=Y | refuted=False | page=p. 6 (methods) and p. 7 (detecting UTC change) | quote="After excluding 532 points as permanent water and 777 points as uninterpretable in at least one year, 8691 points remained for land cover change analysis." (p. 6) … "We tested for statistically significant change within each cover class over 40 years with McNemar tests [17], specifically the 'exact2x2' package in R" … "When we compared a random 5% subsample of points to a second, independent interpreter, we found 91.5% agreement regarding whether a given point was a tree across all years" (p. 7) | reason=I tried to refute this and could not. The change estimate is generated from the SAME 8,691 points interpreted at all five dates — a genuinely paired design that observes per-point transitions (Sankey diagrams of class-to-class flows, p. 9, 11), and McNemar is a matched-pairs test on those pairs, not a two-independent-samples comparison. The 5% second-interpreter check is a RANDOM subsample (not stable-points-only) evaluated 'across all years', and its output is deployed at the change level: eqn. 1 combines both interpreters' cover percentages at t1=1970 and t2=2010 to yield a 3.2 pp 40-year detectable-change threshold that is then applied to every reported change ('No district had detectable levels of UTC loss', p. 9). That is a change-detectability number, not a per-date accuracy number. The criterion's own enumerated qualifier — 'paired points' — is unambiguously present. Two real weaknesses that fall short of refutation: (a) the paired points ARE the estimate, so there is no reference INDEPENDENT of the estimate that sees change; (b) the second-interpreter number is precision/repeatability, not accuracy — it cannot catch a bias both interpreters share, and the archive invites exactly that (1970 'Partially on' leaf status and grayscale 61 cm vs 2010 color 9.7 cm; only 2000 and 2010 orthorectified, p. 6). Nothing in the paper tests whether the +4.3 pp is partly a leaf-status/color/resolution artifact. But 'no independent change reference' is not the failure mode C5 screens for — stable-points-only or no validation is — and neither applies.
- C5 sub-claim — '2008 lidar cross-check' offered as change validation: claimed=part of the C5 Y | skeptic=does not support C5 | refuted=True | page=p. 7 | quote="Comparing our 2010 interpretation to 2008 LiDAR [74], we found 93.4% agreement regarding whether a given point was a tree or not. Most of the disagreement (4.3%) was due to the interpreter classifying points as tree/shrub that were not categorized as tree by LiDAR." | reason=This leg is exactly what the lens says to refute: a SINGLE-DATE state comparison (one epoch, 2010) against ANOTHER MAP PRODUCT (a 2.4 m-height-cutoff lidar land-cover layer), across a two-year offset. It validates no change quantity, has no 1970 (or 1980/1990/2000) counterpart, and its residual disagreement is attributed to a definitional shrub/tree mismatch rather than to interpretation error. C5 survives on paired points plus the eqn. 1 threshold; it gains nothing from the lidar check, and citing the lidar check as change validation is unsupported.
- C1 — aerial sub-meter imagery, multi-vendor/multi-program archive, no radiometric calibration: claimed=Y | skeptic=Y | refuted=False | page=p. 6 (Table 1 and following text) | quote="Table 1. Aerial photos used to assess land cover. Images were obtained from the Pennsylvania Spatial Data Clearinghouse (PASDA) and the Delaware Valley Regional Planning Commission (DVRPC)." … "Our percent uninterpretable in 2000 (1.4%) and 2010 (0.1%)—the only years that were orthorectified" | reason=Outside my lens; not contradicted. All aerial, all sub-meter (61/61/61/46/9.7 cm), two delivery programs, four grayscale + one color, leaf-off vs 'Partially on', three of five not orthorectified, and no radiometric normalization of any kind is described. Heterogeneous multi-program archive: Y stands.
- C2 — ≥5 epochs spanning ≥10 years: claimed=Y | skeptic=Y | refuted=False | page=p. 5 | quote="Our analysis spanned 40 years, with imagery from 1970, 1980, 1990, 2000, and 2010 (Table 1)." | reason=Outside my lens; not contradicted. Exactly 5 epochs over 40 years.
- C3 — segmentation / wall-to-wall mapping, NOT point/dot-grid sampling: claimed=N (10,000 random points) | skeptic=N | refuted=False | page=p. 5 | quote="We used 10,000 random points. This high number enabled us to conduct discrete analyses of different geographic units within the city" | reason=Outside my lens; not contradicted, and confirmed decisively. This is a dot-grid/random-point photointerpretation study with no wall-to-wall product, no polygons, and no segmentation. N stands.
- C4 — temporal inconsistency quantified with a number: claimed=Y (91.5% second-interpreter agreement → 3.2 pp detectable-change threshold) | skeptic=Y | refuted=False | page=p. 8 | quote="Following eqn. 1, any 40-year UTC changes larger than 3.2 percentage points are considered detectable change. For other cover classes, 40-year detectable change thresholds are: 4.7 herbaceous, 7.4 other impervious, 1.2 building, and 9.1 other impervious." | reason=Outside my lens; not contradicted. C4 explicitly accepts a 'detectable-change metric with a number' and 3.2 pp is one, applied throughout Tables 3 and 4. Supporting numbers also exist: 91.5%/92.9%/94.9% inter-interpreter agreement, 1.3 pp average interpreter difference, per-year uninterpretable rates (2.7/6.0/2.7/1.4/0.1%), and persistence figures (65.0% stable tree citywide, 51.9% developed vs 89.3% protected open space, p. 8 and p. 12 Table 3) that function as an inconsistency/flicker-adjacent census. Caveat for the reader: the quantity measures INTERPRETER variability, not sensor/processing inconsistency across dates — the imagery-driven inconsistency that dominates our F3/F4/F7 is never isolated. Y is earned on the criterion's literal wording, not on mechanism parity with our problem.

## City of Toronto (2018 Tree Canopy Study). Prepared by KBM Resources Group / Lallemand-BioForest for the City of Toronto (Automated Land Cover Analysis by Ryan Garnett, City of Toronto IT Division). "2018 Tree Canopy Study" (backgroundfile-173563.pdf), report for action, City of Toronto, 2018 (compares to 2008/2009 baseline).
Reader scores: {"C1": "?", "C2": "Y", "C3": "Y", "C4": "Y", "C5": "Y"} y=6

### Lens: archive-and-epochs (C1, C2) — overall 3, clears_bar=False
SUMMARY: Both criteria in my lens are refuted; Toronto is not the twin. C1: the study's imagery is stated to be SATELLITE, not aerial — Appendix G Table 40 lists tree/grass/bare/shrub as 'Extracted from satellite imagery; assisted with LiDAR', §6.3 calls the inputs 'satellite images for this project', and the 2018 point sample is '2018 leaf-on satellite imagery at 50cm resolution'. No sensor or vendor is ever named, no GSD is given for the mapping imagery, and the words 'radiometric', 'calibrat*', 'normaliz*', and 'histogram match' appear zero times in 262 pages — so neither the multi-vendor/multi-program archive nor the absent-radiometric-calibration condition is supported by text, and the aerial condition is affirmatively contradicted. C2: the wall-to-wall mapping exists at exactly two epochs — Appendix G §9 says the City 'has undertaken a land cover classification twice in the past ten years, once in 2008 and again in 2018', and §8.1 describes change detection as 'a pixel-to-pixel analysis between the two comparative years (2008 and 2018)'. The multi-year years the reader cites (2013, 2014, 2017, 2018) come from a footnote about point-sample imagery vintages — point/dot-grid sampling, explicitly excluded by C3 — and even that is 4 vintages over 5 years, with no 2013 or 2017 canopy value printed anywhere. The plotted trend (Figure 6) is three points (2009/2014/2018) and the widest table (Table 11) is three points (1999/2009/2018), both inherited in part from the 2013 USDA report. Structurally this study is the mirror image of the twin: its multi-epoch stream is point sampling (fails C3) and its mapping stream is two epochs (fails C2), and the two cannot be pooled without inference. I leave C3/C4/C5 at the reader's Y for the count, but flag that C4 is also weak on its face — the reader's own decisive quote (Table 4, report p.32) says reliability of change detection 'cannot be ascertained due to limited replicability of the approach and corresponding differences in cover type classification accuracies, which cannot be measured', i.e. inconsistency is acknowledged and declared unmeasurable, not quantified; if C4 falls too the score is 2/5. Either way, 3/5 does not clear the 4/5 bar.

[Table 40] Tree — Extracted from satellite imagery; assisted with LiDAR ... Toronto states the opposite on both of its data streams: the wall-to-wall land-cover product is derived from 'multispectral satellite imagery' (Appendix G, Table 40, and §6.3 'satellite images for this project'), and the 2018 point sample is from '2018 leaf-on satellite imagery at 50cm resolution'. The only aerial mention is the inherited 2009 baseline ('leaf-on aerial imagery from 2009', fn.31), which belongs to the 2013 USDA report, not to this study's mapping. Beyond the aerial/satellite failure, the archive itself is undocumented: a full-text search finds ZERO occurrences of any sensor or vendor name (no WorldView/GeoEye/QuickBird/Pleiades/First Base/SWOOP/UltraCam/Leica), ZERO statements of the mapping imagery's GSD, and ZERO occurrences of 'radiometric', 'calibrat*', 'normaliz*', 'histogram match', or 'atmospheric correction'. There is no printed evidence of a multi-vendor/multi-program archive and no printed evidence of absent radiometric calibration — a '?' here is generous; the decisive text affirmatively contradicts the 'aerial' half of the criterion, so this is an N.
- C2: claimed=Y | skeptic=N | refuted=True | page=report p.242 (PDF p.243), Appendix G §9 Future Considerations; Figure 6 report p.49 (PDF p.50); Table 11 report p.68 (PDF p.69) | quote=The City of Toronto has undertaken a land cover classification twice in the past ten years, once in 2008 and again in 2018. | reason=Counted exactly as printed, no data stream in this report reaches 5 epochs. (a) The wall-to-wall mapping stream — the only stream that could satisfy C3 — is explicitly TWO epochs: 2008 and 2018, and §8.1 confirms 'a pixel-to-pixel analysis between the two comparative years (2008 and 2018)'. (b) The trend series actually plotted, Figure 6 'Change in canopy in Toronto from 2009-2018', prints THREE values only: 26.6 (2009), 29.1 (2014), 28.4 (2018) — 9 years. (c) The widest printed series, Table 11, is 1999 / 2009 / 2018 — THREE epochs — and is point-sample data inherited from the 2013 USDA report. The reader's C2 quote (fn.8, report p.21: 'high resolution leaf-on orthoimagery for the years 2013, 2014, 2017 and 2018') is not five epochs and is not even four analysis epochs: it is a footnote listing the imagery vintages behind the point-sample estimates, it is point/dot-grid sampling (excluded by C3), it yields at most 2013/2014/2017/2018 = a 5-year span, and no per-year table or figure anywhere in the 262-page document reports separate 2013 or 2017 canopy values. To get 5 epochs one must pool the point-sample vintages with the mapping years across two incompatible methodologies and two different reports — pure inference, not text. Either read alone fails: mapping = 2 epochs / 10 years; point sample = 3 printed epochs (max 19-year span) or 4 imagery vintages over 5 years.

### Lens: method-and-metric (C3, C4) — overall 3, clears_bar=False
SUMMARY: Toronto 2018 fails the twin bar at 3/5 (C2 Y, C3 Y, C5 Y; C4 N, C1 N). C3 survives -- there IS a genuine wall-to-wall product (Appendix G: segmentation of multispectral satellite imagery + LiDAR height thresholds, rasterized to a continuous 8-class land cover raster, used for parcel- and neighbourhood-level tree-cover-change maps) -- but the reader's supporting quote is non-probative (it is Table 4, a generic methodology pros/cons table describing what automated classification can do in principle, not what this study did). C4 is refuted outright: temporal inconsistency is only ACKNOWLEDGED, never quantified. The report says four separate times that change reliability cannot be measured ('accuracy and completeness ... has not been independently validated'; 'no measure of statistical accuracy associated with the 2018 land cover dataset'; 'statistical accuracy not available'; 'it can be difficult to perform a true comparison between two time periods separated by a decade'), and the reader cited that very disclaimer as evidence FOR quantification. Table 41 'Change Detection Matrix' is only a from-to code lookup key, never populated with transition areas; Table 9 is a between-method, tree-vs-tree+shrub apples-to-oranges display, not a spurious-change metric; the 96/89/85% figures are attribution overlays. No flicker rate, no inter-date agreement, no repeat-delivery comparison, no change accuracy assessment exists in 260 pages. The deeper structural failure: the criteria are satisfied by two different, non-interchangeable products. The multi-epoch series (2009, 2013, 2014, 2017, 2018) that carries C2 and every trend number is a 10,000-point dot grid -- exactly what C3 excludes -- while the wall-to-wall product exists for only two epochs (2008, 2018) and is explicitly not canopy-comparable across them because the 2008 grass/shrub class was split in 2018 ('there can be no canopy cover (tree and shrub) comparison using the automated land cover classification method'). Notably, the study exhibits our F1 pathology -- the map says tree cover fell 28%->26% while the point sample says canopy rose 26.6%->28.4% -- and simply narrates past it rather than measuring it. Useful as a cautionary exemplar of the disease, worthless as a twin that diagnoses it.

Advanced classification algorithms and processing were applied to produce classified Land Cover datasets. ... In 2008, the total tree cover was 28%. In 2018 the tree cover decreased by 2% to 26% | reason=C3 survives, but NOT on the reader's evidence. The reader's quote is from Table 4 (report p.32), a generic textbook pros/cons comparison of point sampling vs. automated classification -- it describes what the automated method CAN do in general, not what this study produced; it is non-probative. The real support is elsewhere: Appendix G documents an actual wall-to-wall product (PCI Object Analyst segmentation of multispectral satellite imagery + LiDAR height rules, 'Land cover tree class = when LiDAR max height >=1.5; LiDAR mean height >= 0.75 AND NDVI mean > 0.5', rasterized into 'a single continuous land cover dataset'), and it is used for spatially explicit change products (Table 6 ravine tree cover ha, Figure 13 tree cover change by neighbourhood, Figures 7-11 parcel-level tree cover loss overlays, Table 35 neighbourhood tree cover change). So a wall-to-wall canopy product exists. Two heavy caveats that gut its value as a twin: (a) the wall-to-wall product spans exactly TWO epochs -- 'The City of Toronto has undertaken a land cover classification twice in the past ten years, once in 2008 and again in 2018' (report p.242) -- so it cannot carry C2; the 5-epoch series (2013, 2014, 2017, 2018 + 2009) is 10,000-point dot-grid sampling, precisely what C3 excludes; and (b) the two wall-to-wall epochs are not even class-comparable for canopy: 'In 2008, shrub and grass cover were one land cover class ... For this reason, there can be no canopy cover (tree and shrub) comparison using the automated land cover classification method' (report p.57). No single product in this study satisfies C2 + C3 + C5 together; the reader's 'Y' row is a splice across two different, non-interchangeable products.

For these reasons it can be difficult to perform a true comparison between two time periods separated by a decade. | reason=This is the textbook definition of ACKNOWLEDGED-not-quantified. The report states repeatedly that the inconsistency cannot be measured, and then does not measure it: 'The accuracy and completeness of the 2018 Automated Land Cover classification data has not been independently validated by the study team' (p.195); 'There is no measure of statistical accuracy associated with the 2018 land cover dataset' (fn.33, p.51); 'Based on 2009 and 2018 City of Toronto land cover data change detection; statistical accuracy not available' (fn.23, p.42). The reader's own second C4 quote -- 'Reliability of change detection ... cannot be ascertained ... corresponding differences in cover type classification accuracies, which cannot be measured' -- is evidence AGAINST C4, not for it; the reader cited the disclaimer of quantification as the quantification. I checked every remaining candidate and none is an inter-date consistency metric: (1) Table 9 (the reader's other quote) is a between-METHOD discrepancy at one date pair (-24.9 pp point sample vs -3.3 pp automated for Playter Estates-Danforth), and the report explicitly attributes it to a definitional mismatch -- point sample = tree AND shrub, automated = 'tree' class only -- so it is an apples-to-oranges display, not a spurious-change measurement, and no flicker/disagreement rate is ever computed from it. (2) Table 41 'Change Detection Matrix' (report p.241) is only a power-of-n VALUE LOOKUP KEY for from-to codes (1 minus -16 = -15, etc.); it is never populated with transition areas, and the report states 'The currently available data does not permit an assessment of area (e.g., in square metres or hectares) of tree canopy lost' (p.54). (3) The 96%/89%/85% parcel figures are correlations of loss parcels with ice-storm calls / EAB removals / building permits -- attribution overlays, not consistency metrics -- and are prefaced 'it is not possible to make statistically supportable inferences from these data'. (4) The nearest thing to a detectable-change threshold, 'Statistical significance indicates canopy cover change between sample years where the standard error is relatively low (+/- 5.0 points)' (report p.44), is point-sample SAMPLING error, not map temporal inconsistency -- and in the target problem the human point interpretation is the truth authority (F8), not the noise source. There is no flicker census, no spurious-change rate, no inter-date agreement/IoU, no same-scene repeat comparison, no change accuracy assessment anywhere in 260 pages.

Both wall-to-wall epochs are satellite ('extracted from multispectral satellite imagery, with the assistance of LiDAR information', Table 40, p.235), and the 2018 point sample is also satellite: 'using random point sampling of 10,000 points from 2018 leaf-on satellite imagery at 50cm resolution' (fn.31). Only the 2009 baseline point sample used 'leaf-on aerial imagery'. There is also no heterogeneous multi-vendor archive here -- it is two bespoke city procurements a decade apart, plus city-wide LiDAR. C1 is N, not '?'.

### Lens: validation-design (C5) — overall 3, clears_bar=False
SUMMARY: C5 REFUTED. Toronto 2018 produces two change estimates and validates neither. The wall-to-wall product that earns C3 (2008-vs-2018 automated land cover, pixel-to-pixel change matrix in Appendix G section 8) is explicitly unvalidated -- 'has not been independently validated by the study team' (Appendix E, report p.195), 'no measure of statistical accuracy' (fn.33, report p.51), 'Reliability of change detection ... cannot be ascertained' (Table 4, report p.32). No accuracy assessment, error matrix, or reference sample appears anywhere in 262 pages. The 10,000-point photo-interpretation is not a validation of that map: the report says the class definitions make the two non-comparable (report p.57), the baseline years differ (2009 vs 2008), and Table 9 presents them as 'two different estimation methodologies' set side by side disagreeing in sign citywide (+1.8 pp canopy vs -2 pp tree) and by >20 pp at neighbourhood scale, with no reconciliation and no arbiter. The point sample is also itself unvalidated and unpaired -- two independent 10,000-point draws (2009 by USDA FS on leaf-on AERIAL imagery, 2018 on 50 cm leaf-on SATELLITE imagery, fn.31), differenced as independent proportions, with no interpreter QA and no co-located points. Overall Y count = 3 of 5 (C2, C3, C4 taken from the reader), below the 4/5 bar. Notes outside my lens, not rescored: (a) C1 is clearly N rather than '?' -- both the mapping ('extracted from multispectral satellite imagery', Appendix G Table 40) and the 2018 point sample ('2018 leaf-on satellite imagery at 50cm', fn.31) are satellite, not aerial, and there is no multi-vendor aerial archive; (b) C4's Y rests on a between-METHOD discrepancy at a single 10-year interval plus a prose statement that reliability 'cannot be measured' -- not an inter-date/flicker/spurious-change metric; the C4 reviewer should test that; (c) C2's five epochs (2009/2013/2014/2017/2018) come only from footnote 8 with no methodology given, and only three (2009, 2014, 2018) appear in Figure 6, while the MAPPING has just two epochs (2008, 2018). Consistency check for the orchestrator: Roman 2021 Philadelphia was scored C5=Y on a point-sample change estimate; the discriminator against Toronto should be whether Roman re-interpreted FIXED points across dates (a paired, change-capable design) rather than differencing independent draws from two separate studies as Toronto does -- if Roman is also unpaired-independent, one of the two scores needs revisiting.
- C5: claimed=Y | skeptic=N | refuted=True | page=report p.57 (PDF p.58); Appendix E, report p.195 (PDF p.196); footnote 33, report p.51 (PDF p.52) | quote=The accuracy and completeness of the 2018 Automated Land Cover classification data has not been independently validated by the study team. ... [fn.33] There is no measure of statistical accuracy associated with the 2018 land cover dataset. ... [p.57] In 2008, shrub and grass cover were one land cover class, representing 'canopy cover'. In 2018, grass and shrub cover were separate land cover classes. For this reason, there can be no 'canopy cover' (tree and shrub) comparison using the automated land cover classification method. | reason=Change estimates exist, but NO validation of any kind exists for either of them. (1) The spatially explicit change product -- the 2008-vs-2018 pixel-to-pixel 'power of n' change-detection matrix in Appendix G section 8, and the Tree-only 28%->26% (-2 pp) figure in Appendix E -- is explicitly unvalidated: 'has not been independently validated by the study team', 'no measure of statistical accuracy', and Table 4 (report p.32) 'Reliability of change detection, especially at smaller scales, cannot be ascertained ... classification accuracies, which cannot be measured.' There is no accuracy assessment, no confusion/error matrix, no reference sample anywhere in the 262-page document (grep for 'accuracy assessment', 'overall accuracy', 'error matrix', 'confusion matrix', 'paired' returns nothing). (2) The only Y-preserving reading is that the 10,000-point photo-interpretation serves as an independent change reference validating the map. The report itself forecloses that: the classes are not comparable ('there can be no canopy cover (tree and shrub) comparison using the automated land cover classification method'), the baselines differ (point sample 2009 vs land cover 2008), and Table 9's own caption frames them as 'two different estimation methodologies' -- co-equal alternatives, never estimate-plus-validation. Neither is used to adjudicate the other; they are set side by side disagreeing in sign citywide (+1.8 pp canopy vs -2 pp tree cover) and by >20 pp at neighbourhood scale (Playter Estates-Danforth -24.9 vs -3.3), with zero reconciliation. (3) The point-sample change is itself an unvalidated primary estimate, not a validation design: it is a difference of two INDEPENDENT random draws, not re-interpretation of fixed points -- 'In 2009, 10,000 random sample points were generated ... In 2018, 10,000 random sample points were generated for the same purpose' (report p.56) -- and the two draws come from different studies on different sensor classes (fn.31: 2009 'leaf-on aerial imagery' reported by the USDA Forest Service in 2013, vs 2018 'leaf-on satellite imagery at 50cm resolution'). There is no interpreter QA, no blind re-interpretation, no co-located points, no change-stratified sample. So: a trend number exists, but nothing in the study can see whether either change estimate is right.

### Lens: archive-and-epochs (C1, C2) — overall 3, clears_bar=False
SUMMARY: Lens verdict (C1, C2): neither of my two criteria is refuted. C1 was already scored non-Y by the reader and I concur — indeed more strongly than the reader did. The body text on p.647 ('All panchromatic ... photos collected in 1938, 1957, and 1971 were first scanned and then individually georectified to the 1999 orthophotos' vs. the 1999/2004 CIR being 0.6 m and orthorectified) unscrambles the OCR-mangled Table 1 and pins the three georectified GSDs (0.86, 1.0, 1.0) to 1938/1957/1971 — so 1971 is 1.0 m, not the 0.6 m the reader read, and two of five imagery epochs are flatly not sub-meter. On top of that, no vendor, agency, program, or delivery chain is named anywhere in the paper, radiometric calibration is never mentioned in any form, and the sixth epoch (1914) is a scanned hard-copy state-forester map digitized at a 2 ha MMU, not aerial imagery at all. Multi-vendor/multi-program status would be pure inference. C2 is a clean Y and survives even the strictest adversarial trim: excluding the 1914 map still leaves five aerial epochs spanning 66 years (1938-2004), well past the ≥5 / ≥10 yr bar, though spacing is very uneven with a 28-year 1971-1999 gap.

Load-bearing call: the bar fails ONLY on my out-of-lens flip of C4 from Y to N. C1 and C2 alone do not move the outcome — with the reader's C4 intact the count is 4 and the study clears. I flipped C4 because the reader's evidence is within-epoch inter-INTERPRETER agreement, not inter-DATE consistency, and because the paper reports only the 90% redraw THRESHOLD and never any measured rate; its sole spurious-change treatment (0.5 ha minimum change unit against misregistration slivers) is acknowledgment-plus-mitigation with no quantity attached, which C4 excludes in so many words. The orchestrator should weigh that flip against the dedicated C4 skeptic; if C4 is restored to Y, this paper reads 4/5 and clears on C2/C3/C4/C5 with C1 as the sole miss. Either way C1 is a hard N, so this is at best a 4/5 twin, not a 5/5, and its C1 failure is the same axis on which Pawlak and Roman already fall short.
- C1: claimed=N/? — single coherent historical aerial-photo/map series for one watershed, not stated as multi-vendor/multi-program; GSD mostly 0.6-1.0 m (not clearly sub-meter throughout) and the 1914 epoch is a scanned historic map, not an aerial photo | skeptic=N | refuted=False | page=647-648 | quote=Forest patches in 1914 were digitized from a georegistered digital map scanned from a hard copy of the 1914 forest map created by Fred Besley, Maryland's first state forester... The 1999 and 2004 color-infrared imagery had a spatial resolution of 0.6 m, and was orthorectified. All panchromatic (black-and-white) photos collected in 1938, 1957, and 1971 were first scanned and then individually georectified to the 1999 orthophotos. [Table 1: 1938 (April) Pan 1:20,000 0.86 Georectified; 1957 (October) Pan 1:20,000 1.0 Georectified; 1971 (September) Pan 1:20,000 1.0 Georectified; 1999 (October) CIR 1:10,000 0.6 Orthorectified; 2004 (August) CIR 1:10,000 0.6 Orthorectified] | reason=I concur with the reader's non-Y, and the reader's own GSD reading is if anything too generous. Table 1's OCR is column-scrambled, but the body text disambiguates it exactly: three panchromatic epochs (1938, 1957, 1971) are GEOrectified and two CIR epochs (1999, 2004) are ORTHOrectified — which pins Table 1's three georectified rows (0.86, 1.0, 1.0) to 1938/1957/1971 and the two orthorectified 0.6 m rows to 1999/2004. So 1971 is 1.0 m, not 0.6 m: two of the five imagery epochs sit at exactly 1.0 m, i.e. NOT sub-meter, and a third (1938, 0.86 m) is only marginally so. Beyond resolution, the archive fails the multi-vendor/multi-program test on the text: no acquiring agency, vendor, program, contract, or delivery chain is named anywhere in the paper — Table 1 gives only year, film type, scale, GSD, and process level, and the paper never characterizes its photos as coming from different sources. Multi-vendor status would have to be INFERRED from the mere fact that 1938-2004 photography must have come from somewhere, which is precisely the inference this lens forbids. Radiometric calibration is never mentioned at all — not performed, not discussed, not even acknowledged as an issue; the only cross-date processing described is geometric (image-to-image georeferencing with ≥15 control points). Absence of a calibration statement is compatible with 'without radiometric calibration' but supplies no evidence of a heterogeneous multi-program archive. Finally the 1914 epoch is not imagery at all but a scanned hard-copy forest map by the state forester, digitized at a 2 ha MMU versus 0.5 ha for the photo epochs (p.647), so the sixth 'epoch' is a cartographic product on a different mapping convention. N stands.
- C2: claimed=Y — six epochs (1914,1938,1957,1971,1999,2004) spanning 90 years | skeptic=Y | refuted=False | page=645/647 | quote=Using a database of forest patches from six times--1914, 1938, 1957, 1971, 1999, and 2004--we found that forest cover changed, both temporally and spatially. / A spatial-temporal database including six time slices of forest patches--1914, 1938, 1957, 1971, 1999, and 2004--was created from a historic forest map and aerial photos for the Gwynns Falls watershed using ArcGIS 9.3. | reason=Printed explicitly in both the abstract and the Data section; the epoch list is enumerated verbatim, not inferred, and the span 1914-2004 is 90 years. The criterion is ≥5 epochs over ≥10 years and this passes with margin under every reading I can construct. I deliberately tried the strictest adversarial reading — drop 1914 because it is a scanned map rather than an aerial photo (the same defect that sinks C1) — and C2 still survives: five aerial epochs, 1938/1957/1971/1999/2004, spanning 66 years. The epoch spacing is very uneven (19, 14, 28, 5 years) and one interval, 1971-1999, is a 28-year gap during which no intermediate map exists, but C2 as written imposes no spacing requirement, only a count and a span. No refutation available.
- C4: claimed=Y — quantified inter-interpreter consistency rate with a 90% redraw threshold | skeptic=N | refuted=True | page=648/649 | quote=Photo interpretation was conducted by two trained interpreters, and validated by one quality controller to reduce the inconsistency of the interpretation between the two interpreters (Teixeira et al. 2009; Gerard et al. 2010). Patches were redrawn for improvement when the consistency rate was below 90% (Gerard et al. 2010). / In addition, using 0.5 ha as the minimum change mapping unit can greatly reduce ``false changes'' caused by errors of misregistration between aerial photos collected in different years. | reason=Outside lens; flagged because I read the full text and the contradiction is textual, not interpretive, so the orchestrator should weigh it against the dedicated C4 skeptic. C4 requires TEMPORAL inconsistency quantified — flicker, spurious change, inter-DATE consistency, or a detectable-change metric carrying an actual number. The reader's quote is inter-INTERPRETER agreement WITHIN a single epoch's photo interpretation: two people drawing the same year's photos. That is a production-QA rule about draftsmanship, not a measurement of how much apparent change between dates is artifactual. Worse, no measured value is ever reported: 90% is the THRESHOLD below which patches were redrawn, so the paper's own procedure guarantees the final consistency rate is somewhere ≥90% and never prints what it was, for any epoch. The only other candidate is the 0.5 ha minimum change mapping unit, and the paper's own wording places it squarely in the category the criterion excludes verbatim — misregistration-driven sliver polygons are ACKNOWLEDGED and MITIGATED by a threshold, never quantified; the paper reports no count, area, or fraction of false changes, before or after. The one number that superficially resembles a flicker statistic — 301 of 807 ha (37.5%) of forest regenerated 1938-1957 removed again by 1971 (p.656) — is presented as genuine ecological turnover and used as evidence for the paper's argument, not as an inconsistency estimate; reading it as flicker quantification would invert the authors' own claim. Under the default-to-refuted rule, N.

### Lens: method-and-metric (C3, C4) — overall 2, clears_bar=False
SUMMARY: Within my lens, C3 survives and C4 falls. C3 = Y: Zhou et al. 2011 is a genuine wall-to-wall manually digitized forest-patch polygon layer at six epochs (MMU 0.5 ha; 2 ha in 1914), not a point or dot-grid sample — though the Table 2 digitizing rules (exclude single rows of trees, exclude forest between houses, patches must be >=100 x 50 m) deliberately delete the dispersed, edge-concentrated canopy that is our actual signal, so it clears C3 by letter while being a poor methodological twin. C4 = N, refuted: the claimed 90% is an inter-INTERPRETER redraw trigger applied within a single date, with no achieved value ever reported and no comparison across dates — per-date interpretation quality, exactly what the lens says to refute. The only cross-date artifact statement is that a 0.5 ha minimum change unit 'can greatly reduce false changes' from misregistration — unquantified, with the threshold inherited from the MMU rather than derived from a measured registration error (15 control points, no RMSE). The one flicker-shaped number (37.5% of 1938-57 regeneration re-removed by 1971) is presented as real land-use turnover, never partitioned into signal versus artifact. Temporal inconsistency is acknowledged and filtered, never measured. I also flipped C5 to N as a clear textual contradiction reachable from this lens: the paper contains no validation design of any kind — no reference data, no accuracy assessment, and attribution performed by re-inspecting the same photos that made the map. Using the reader's C1 (N/?) and C2 (Y): overall 2/5, does not clear. Strictly in-lens (leaving C5 at the reader's Y) it would be 3/5 and still fail to clear.
- C3: claimed=Y — wall-to-wall forest-patch polygon database at each epoch via photointerpretation/digitizing, not point sampling | skeptic=Y | refuted=False | page=647 | quote=Forest patches from 1938 to 2004 were interpreted and digitized from aerial photos based on a series of rules that were defined for forest patch delineation (Table 2). The minimum mapping unit was 0.5 ha (i.e., only forest patches with sizes larger than 0.5 ha were mapped), smaller than the 2 ha used for the 1914 map. | reason=Confirmed: the product is a continuous manually digitized polygon layer covering the whole 17,150 ha watershed at every epoch, with a stated MMU, not a dot grid or point sample. It meets the letter of C3. Caveat that weakens it as a twin without changing the score: this is forest-PATCH mapping, not canopy mapping. Table 2 (p648) rules explicitly discard exactly the material F6 says carries the real signal — 'Exclude just one row of trees'; 'Exclude the extensions of forest in between the houses, and draw a straight line in back of the houses'; 'Exclude an opening in the forest canopy only if it is greater than 20 m by 20 m'; and a patch must be >=100 m long and >=50 m wide. A dispersed, edge-concentrated canopy signal (verified losses median 2.0 m from a crown edge; largest loss cluster 0.41 ha) is definitionally invisible to this product. Also note the 1914 epoch uses a 2 ha MMU from a scanned Besley map, so the layer is not even internally consistent in its own mapping rule across epochs.
- C4: claimed=Y — quantified inter-interpreter consistency rate with a 90% redraw threshold | skeptic=N | refuted=True | page=648 | quote=Photo interpretation was conducted by two trained interpreters, and validated by one quality controller to reduce the inconsistency of the interpretation between the two interpreters (Teixeira et al. 2009; Gerard et al. 2010). Patches were redrawn for improvement when the consistency rate was below 90% (Gerard et al. 2010). | reason=This is precisely the failure mode the lens says to refute: the Y rests on per-date interpretation quality, not on temporal inconsistency. Three points. (1) The 90% figure is a within-date, between-PERSON QC redraw trigger applied to one epoch's delineation; it never compares one date's polygons to another date's, so it cannot be an inter-date consistency metric. It is also a procedural threshold, not a result — no achieved consistency value is reported for any epoch anywhere in the paper, so there is no number to read as a measurement. (2) The only cross-date statement about spurious change is p649: 'using 0.5 ha as the minimum change mapping unit can greatly reduce "false changes" caused by errors of misregistration between aerial photos collected in different years.' 'Greatly reduce' is unquantified; the 0.5 ha value is simply inherited from the mapping MMU, not derived from any measured registration error (p648 states only 'a minimum of 15 control points' with no RMSE reported). That is a mitigation applied blind — inconsistency acknowledged and filtered, never measured. (3) I considered the one flicker-shaped number in the paper, p656: '301 ha out of the 807 ha (or 37.5%) of forest cover newly regenerated between 1938 and 1957 was again later removed between 1957 and 1971.' It does not rescue C4, because the paper presents it as genuine land-use turnover attributed to development, with no attempt to partition it into real change versus delineation/registration artifact. Nothing in this paper would tell you what fraction of its mapped 'change' is noise.
- C5: claimed=Y — from-to transition analysis explicitly designed to detect and attribute dispersed change (citywide and by distance band), reporting <20% of 1914 forest cover unchanged by 2004 | skeptic=N | refuted=True | page=649 | quote=Locations were identified as deforested if forest cover occurred in the earlier year, but not in the later year; whereas sites were defined as reforested if forest cover did not occur in the earlier year, but existed in the later year. We only identified locations with changes in patch size larger than 0.5 ha, which is the minimum mapping unit for patch delineation in this study. | reason=Flagged as a clear contradiction visible from inside my lens, since C4 and C5 share the same missing apparatus. The reader's quote is the change-DETECTION rule (the definition of deforested/reforested), not a validation design. There is no validation section, no reference dataset, no accuracy assessment, no confidence interval, and no independent interpretation anywhere in the 15 pages; the attribution step (p649, 'each patch was investigated to identify the nature of the land cover transitions ... by locating the patch on the aerial photos') reuses the very same photos and interpreters that produced the map, so it is map self-inspection, not validation. C5 requires a change estimate produced WITH a validation design that can see change; here there is no validation design at all, which is a stricter failure than the stable-points-only case C5 was written to exclude. Absence is textual rather than interpretive, so I flip it despite it sitting outside my assigned lens.

### Lens: validation-design (C5) — overall 2, clears_bar=False
SUMMARY: Zhou et al. 2011 fails C5. It produces a rich change/trend product (five successive from-to transition analyses, loss/gain rates by 3 km distance band, <20% of 1914 forest persisting to 2004) but subjects none of it to validation: no reference sample, no paired points, no change-stratified sample, no independent change reference, no accuracy or error statistic anywhere in the paper. The strongest-looking candidate — manual inspection of every >0.5 ha change polygon on the aerial photos (p.649) — is the map-production attribution step, run by the same interpreters on the same imagery that produced the polygons; it can only characterize changes the maps already declared, is structurally blind to omission, and returns no number. The only thing called 'validated' (p.648) is a within-date QC reconciliation between two interpreters, i.e. per-date agreement, which the lens explicitly excludes. The 0.5 ha change threshold is a false-change SUPPRESSOR, not a false-change MEASUREMENT. I also flag C4 as a clear contradiction outside my lens: the 90% figure is an inter-analyst redraw threshold, not a reported inter-date/temporal-inconsistency number, and no achieved value is given; the 37.5% regenerate-then-removed figure (p.656) is reported as real turnover, not as spurious change. Keeping the reader's C1 (N/?), C2 (Y) and C3 (Y): Y-count = 2/5, well below the 4/5 bar. Relative to the problem, this study is closest in spirit to F6/F8 concerns but supplies no validated change estimate and no temporal-noise floor — it is the pre-measurement condition the twin search is trying to escape, not a twin.
- C5: claimed=Y — from-to transition analysis explicitly designed to detect and attribute dispersed change (citywide and by distance band), reporting <20% of 1914 forest cover unchanged by 2004 | skeptic=N | refuted=True | page=649 (decisive); 648 (corroborating) | quote=We only identified locations with changes in patch size larger than 0.5 ha, which is the minimum mapping unit for patch delineation in this study. In addition, using 0.5 ha as the minimum change mapping unit can greatly reduce ``false changes'' caused by errors of misregistration between aerial photos collected in different years. ... Once patches that changed by greater than 0.5 ha were identified, each patch was investigated to identify the nature of the land cover transitions. This was done by locating the patch on the aerial photos and identifying the land cover at that location for each time slice. [p.648:] Photo interpretation was conducted by two trained interpreters, and validated by one quality controller to reduce the inconsistency of the interpretation between the two interpreters ... Patches were redrawn for improvement when the consistency rate was below 90%. | reason=A change/trend estimate is certainly produced (Tables 5-7, Figs 7-11), but the paper contains NO validation of it — no reference sample of any kind, no accuracy figure, no error bar, no omission/commission rate, not even a stable-points-only assessment. The reader's own quotes are (a) the operational DEFINITION of deforested/reforested and (b) a RESULT (<20% unchanged); neither is a validation design. The one passage a defender would cite — every >0.5 ha change polygon 'investigated ... by locating the patch on the aerial photos' (p.649) — is the from-to ATTRIBUTION step of map production, performed by the same interpreters on the same source photos that generated the polygons. It labels what each already-detected change was, and by construction can only inspect commissions the maps already flagged; it is structurally blind to omitted change and yields no independent reference and no quantity. The word 'validated' occurs exactly once in the paper (p.648) and refers to a within-date QC controller reconciling two interpreters' delineations — per-date consistency, not change. The 0.5 ha minimum change unit is explicitly a filter to SUPPRESS false change, not a measurement of it, and its adequacy is never tested. Under the lens rule (refute Y scores resting on per-date agreement or on no independent change reference), this is a clear N.
- C4: claimed=Y — quantified inter-interpreter consistency rate with a 90% redraw threshold | skeptic=N (OUTSIDE MY LENS — flagged as clear contradiction, not a lens ruling) | refuted=True | page=648 | quote=Photo interpretation was conducted by two trained interpreters, and validated by one quality controller to reduce the inconsistency of the interpretation between the two interpreters (Teixeira et al. 2009; Gerard et al. 2010). Patches were redrawn for improvement when the consistency rate was below 90% (Gerard et al. 2010). | reason=The 90% is a within-date, between-ANALYST QC pass bar (redraw if below), not a reported measurement of TEMPORAL inconsistency between dates; no achieved consistency value is ever given for any epoch or epoch pair. The one genuinely temporal-inconsistency hazard the paper names — misregistration slivers producing 'false changes' (p.649) — is acknowledged and filtered but never quantified. Preempting the strongest counter: p.656 reports '301 ha out of the 807 ha (or 37.5%) of forest cover newly regenerated between 1938 and 1957 was again later removed between 1957 and 1971' — a flicker-shaped figure, but the paper presents it as genuine ecological turnover attributed to development, explicitly not as spurious change or inter-date error, so it does not satisfy C4's requirement of a flicker/spurious-change/inter-date consistency metric. Does not change clears_bar either way.

  "Burnicki, A.C.  "verdict_risk": "\"HIGH — and the first component is not a risk but an already-realized contradiction. (1) THE HEADLINE IS FALSIFIED BY THIS ROUND'S OWN RECORD. Round 2's adversarial verification returned clears=false on all three lenses for BOTH twins. Pawlak entered at C1 Y / C2 N / C3 Y / C4 Y(weak) / C5 Y; the lenses refuted C1, C4 and C5 independently, each reporting 'drops to 3/5' only under the assumption that the other criteria held. Applied jointly, only C3 survives: Pawlak is 1/5, not 4/5. Roman fails C3 by definition (a dot-grid sample is the exact product type C3 excludes) and had C1 refuted: 3/5, not 4/5. The defensible statement is therefore 'ZERO candidates verified at >=4/5; Condition A NOT MET,' and reporting '4/5 and 4/5' would be publishing a number the hunt's own verification already overturned. Note this cuts BOTH ways for credibility: the honest restatement is a stronger, more defensible negative than the current one, not a weaker one. (2) The scoring substrate is unreliable — the 'y' aggregate field is incoherent (Pontius y=28 on a 5-point scale, Toronto y=6, Zhou 2011 y=4 against lens scores of 3/2/2), and Toronto's C2=Y looks wrong on its face (two epochs). Any '>=4/5' or 'no 5/5' claim inherits that unreliability. (3) EVERY ROUND-2 NEGATIVE IS A WEAK NEGATIVE. OpenAlex returned $0-budget errors on all 16 sweeps; the sweeps themselves document never-run queries (Nguyen-2024 forward citations, all 8 non-English-Europe title searches, four proceedings venues, type:dissertation) and state that Crossref is not a viable substitute. 'Nobody has published this' cannot be asserted from a round in which the primary abstract-index engine never once responded. (4) SIX-PLUS DIRECTLY ON-TOPIC PAPERS REMAIN UNREAD, one of them (Richardson & Moskal 2014, Seattle) sitting in the same metropolitan imagery archive as the subject city and titled for exactly the uncertainty question at issue — and several are gold or hybrid CC-BY, i.e. retrieval failures rather than access barriers, with obvious untried routes (Treesearch, TSpace, CFS Publications, the Chrome browser session). (5) 'CONDITION B NOT MET' IS A CATEGORY ERROR AS CURRENTLY FRAMED. It reports that three internally generated designs were judged fatal; it is being read as a finding about the literature. Three solution frames that map directly onto F3/F4/F8 — occupancy/imperfect-detection trend models, official-statistics bridge/overlap designs (the 2017 triple and F7 pair ARE a bridge sample), and threshold-free soft-probability area estimation — were never searched at all. NET: the negative on Condition A is probably TRUE but is currently stated with the wrong numbers and on inadequate evidence; the negative on Condition B is undertested and premature. Recommended posture until Tasks 1-2 and 4-6 are complete: report Condition A as NOT MET with zero verified >=4/5 candidates (an honest, stronger negative), and downgrade Condition B from 'not met' to 'not established — three solution frames unsearched.'\""
}
