 "total": 16,
 "fatal_flaw": "The glue claim does not hold across the partition it is used on. L_pdi establishes p(x|y) invariance across DELIVERY CHAINS WITHIN A DATE (every pair shares date, radiometry, season, and roughly GSD by construction). BCTS+Saerens EM+inference adjustment is then applied ACROSS DATES, and Liu 2022 p.3's precondition (\"a presumption for rectifying the classifier bias using Eq. (4) is the data conditional distribution is well aligned\") is stated over the domains the correction operates on — here, dates. Nothing in TDI aligns p(x|y) across dates. So F4's measured within-sensor cross-year greenness gradient (recall -.109, a whole-distribution radiometric shift) and the Feb-Oct season shift both flow straight into EM, which absorbs residual conditional drift as an apparent prior shift and returns a plausible, confident q_t. And q_t IS the reported canopy fraction for the 35 unlabeled dates — the trend estimate for most of the archive is self-generated by the step whose assumption is violated, with no external anchor. Field consequence: you spend the budget, pass every stated kill (all three are within-2017 or within-the-F7-flight), and ship a SMOOTH, confident, wrong 20-year trend with an authoritative-looking minimum-detectable-change floor stapled to it. That is worse on a real delivery than F1's sawtooth, which at least advertises its own unreliability — a city council cannot un-see a clean line. The design names this weakness honestly in self_assessed_weakest_point, but naming it does not license shipping the trend, and the kill set was not extended to catch it even though a permitted cross-date test (the 2005-2016 lidar CHANGE, already in the design at step 10 as \"sanity only\", and not circular since lidar touches no training) was sitting right there.",
 "verdict": "Do not build as-is; harvest two pieces. Strong runner-up, and the best-argued Condition-B design I have scored on the operating-point axis — it is genuinely the only one that both repairs F3's miscalibrated near-binary rasters (BCTS) and assigns 37 per-date thresholds with no per-date reference (EM+IA), and its refusal of a domain-adversarial head (step 4a) is a correct and non-obvious call given program is confounded with year. It cleanly clears the hard exclusions: no cross-date radiometric transform anywhere, lidar deletable without changing a single number in steps 1-9, no temporal term and no inheritance so F6's dispersed edge loss stays visible. Compute is comfortably in budget — ~14k inference tiles at 512x512 across 37 acquisitions is roughly an hour on one card, not the 1.5 GPU-days claimed, so the estimate is conservative in the right direction; human cost is understated (registration engineering and 37-date EM diagnostic review is nearer a week than 3 days) but same order. Where it loses on my lens: (a) step 2's rejection rule is underspecified on the step the design itself calls decisive — 1.5 px on a 0.5 m grid is a 0.75 m threshold, BELOW the loss's own 2 m tolerance, and the design never says whether the residual is measured on ground tie points only or on all features. If all features, then tiles with the most crown-edge ortho-DEM parallax are systematically rejected and the pair bank is biased toward open ground, i.e. toward exactly where deliveries do not disagree; tie-point matching over canopy is unstable and self-similar, so this is the likely outcome, and the loss then trains on the easy half of the city. (b) No pair member is 1 m NAIP, so nothing supervises the upsampled-NAIP regime that is plausibly a third of the 37 epochs, and the design offers no mitigation and no diagnostic for it. (c) sigma_chain rests on one flight plus one triple — an n~2 basis for a published floor that a city will quote for a decade, and the design correctly notes the bias direction is toward false confidence. Verdict: the trend deliverable is unlicensed as designed; the measurement apparatus is excellent and belongs in whatever wins.",
 "best_idea_to_graft": "Two pieces, both evaluation-side (the safe half), both zero-training-cost and model-agnostic. (1) THE FOUND ZERO-CHANGE BENCHMARK AND PUBLISHED FLOOR. The archive already contains its own traveling subjects — the F7 pair (one flight, two chains) and the 2017 triple — where true canopy is identical by construction, so every pixel of disagreement is pure processing-chain noise with zero temporal confound. Make clearing that benchmark a gate any candidate method must pass, and convert the surviving same-date matched fraction disagreement (sigma_chain) into a minimum-detectable-trend floor published alongside the estimate, never a bare number. This is precisely the in-domain move Roman 2021 makes for interpreter disagreement (\"we propose a detectable change threshold as follows: ... any 40-year UTC changes larger than 3.2 percentage points are considered detectable change\", p.7-8), extended to processing-chain disagreement, and it is the single highest-value-per-dollar idea in this design: it costs no GPU time, needs no new labels, requires no new imagery, and it would have caught the -2.21 pp sign flip as unpublishable rather than as a finding. Note the reframe: TDI's own precedent, DeepBVC, used its 60 same-day pairs as a test-retest EVALUATION set (its consistency loss ran on synthetic augmentations, not real repeat chains, and it states real back-to-back inconsistent scans \"would be required\" and were unavailable). Graft the benchmark half; the training half is the speculative half. (2) THE ON/OFF ATTRIBUTION KILL. TDI's second primary kill — rerun identical architecture, labels and calibration with the novel term switched OFF, and declare the angle dead unless it beats the ablation by >=0.3 pp or >=25% of the ablated gap — is the correct kill-criterion template for every novel angle in this pool. It separates \"the pipeline passed\" from \"the new idea did anything,\" which is the failure mode that lets a dead angle survive review. Any winner should adopt this pattern verbatim, and should extend its kill set with a cross-date test (2005-2016 lidar CHANGE, permitted under the two-epoch rule) so the kills can actually reach the cross-year drift that within-year tests structurally cannot see.",
 "source_check": "Read and grep-verified 15 of the 21 cited quotes verbatim in the cited files; all located, none fabricated, none materially misquoted. Confirmed at exact strings: r2_15_deepbvc.txt line 266-267 (\"the two scans from the same day and subject were used as a longitudinal pair with no atrophy. We used 60 pairs in total (20 pairs per subject)\" and \"scanned back-to-back (i.e. the brain volume difference should be 0%)\"), line 219 (\"to render the model insensitive to differences in imaging quality during training, we adopted a consistency regularisation loss\"), line 467 (\"Consistency regularisation renders the predictions invariant to noise applied to the input\"); r2_38_deepharmony.txt line 303 (12 subjects scanned twice within 30 days on two Philips Achieva 3T scanners) and line 596 (cGM volume / protocol effect); alexandari_bcts.txt line 28 (\"first, calibrate predictions using the held-out training set, then perform domain adaptation on the calibrated predictions using EM\") and the BCTS class-specific-bias passage; undoing_label_shift_seg.txt line 90 (the p(x|y)-alignment presumption), line 184 (Inference Adjustment), line 45 (\"may lead to the phenomenon of misalignment\"); r2_25_xing.txt line 767 (SA \"less sensitive to the interpreters selected\"); roman2021_land_phila.txt lines 242 and 258 (detectable change threshold; 3.2 pp); nguyen2024_swissalps.txt line 297 (\"forest cover remains mainly unchanged between consecutive acquisitions\") and line 390 (gradient scaling \"relaxes the alignment constraint in the case of forest loss\"); r2_36_haca3.txt line 899 (CV to quantify inter-scanner variability) and line 1043 (FTHP, single subject, 116 sites); r2_28_bautin_mdc.txt line 18 (\"simulating scan-rescan experiments\") and line 382 (COVs 0.8% T1w / 0.57% T2w — matches the cited p.5 body text; the abstract rounds T2w to 0.6%, not an error); r2_35_scanner_invariant_mri.txt line 36 (auxiliary loss \"designed to encourage the model to ignore domain-specific information\"). The one unpaginated citation (DeepHarmony HTML, cited by section heading rather than page) is honestly flagged as such in the design. ONE OVER-READ, not a misquote: the design's source note for the DeepBVC pair quote claims it as \"the precedent for using the F7 pair and the 2017 triple as zero-temporal-confound SUPERVISION.\" Line 266 is the Maclaren test-retest EVALUATION set, and DeepBVC's actual consistency loss (line 219) compares original vs synthetically AUGMENTED images, not two real acquisition chains — line 520 states outright that real back-to-back inconsistently-acquired scans \"would be required to complete the test-retest study in the absence of simulated data,\" i.e. they did not have them. So the cited precedent supports same-occasion pairs as a zero-change BENCHMARK, and does not support them as a training pair bank. That is a stretched inference from an accurately transcribed quote, so I hold source_fidelity at 2, but it is exactly why the graftable half of TDI is the evaluation half."
}

### JUDGMENT: {
 "design_name": "Traveling-Delivery Invariance (TDI)",
 "scores": {
  "no_radiometric_calibration": 2,
  "no_extra_lidar": 2,
  "operating_point": 2,
  "not_smoothing_not_inheritance": 2,
  "scales": 2,
  "kill_criterion": 1,
  "novelty": 2,
  "implementability": 2,
  "source_fidelity": 2
 },
 "total": 17,
 "source_check": "I read every cited file and grepped every quote. 21/21 quotes are VERBATIM — none fabricated, none paraphrased-as-quoted. 19/21 sit on exactly the cited page (verified by mapping line numbers to the extractions' \"===== PAGE n\" markers). Verified at exact page: DeepBVC p4 \"render the model insensitive to differences in imaging quality\" (line 219), p5 \"the two scans from the same day and subject were used as a longitudinal pair with no atrophy\" + \"the brain volume difference should be 0%\" (lines 266-267, a two-sentence splice across a sentence boundary but contiguous and faithful), p11 \"renders the predictions invariant to noise\" (line 467); HACA3 p8 CV quote (line 899), p16 FTHP 116-sites quote (line 1043); Alexandari p2 both quotes (lines 28, 31); Liu p3 \"a presumption for rectifying the classifier bias...\" (line 90), p2 \"phenomenon of misalignment\" (line 45); Aslani p2 (lines 36-38, quote spans a page-internal column break, faithful); Bautin p1 (line 18); Roman p7 \"we propose a detectable change threshold as follows:\" (line 242) and p8 \"3.2 percentage points\" (line 258); Nguyen p7 (line 297) and p8 (line 390); Xing p11 (line 767). DeepHarmony's two quotes verify verbatim (lines 303, 596) under the section headings \"2.1. Data\" (line 302) and \"3.4. Longitudinal Stability\" (line 585) — and the design HONESTLY declares that file unpaginated rather than inventing page numbers, which is the correct auditor behavior. I also spot-verified secondary factual claims: Protocol #2 really is DeepHarmony's target chain (line 313/324), so \"DeepHarmony harmonizes toward Protocol #2; L_pdi is symmetric\" is accurate; Roman's \"93.4% agreement / 4.3% disagreement from the height cut-off\" used in step 10 is accurate (line 241).\n\nTHREE DEFECTS, none of them a fabricated quote:\n(1) MIS-ATTRIBUTED METHODOLOGICAL MOVE — Bautin. Step 8 claims it makes \"the same move Bautin makes from scan-rescan variability to a minimum detectable atrophy (p.1).\" Bautin does NOT derive a minimum detectable atrophy threshold. Its p.1 abstract derives a minimum SAMPLE SIZE at a stipulated atrophy (\"The minimum sample size required to detect an atrophy of 2%... was 467 +/- 13.9\"), and the unpaired formula (sec. 2.3.1, line 179) is driven by the INTER-SUBJECT standard deviation sigma_s, with scan-rescan COV entering only as one term. TDI has n=1 city and one measurement per date — there is no population to enlarge, so Bautin's move does not transfer at all. The quotes are real; the claim label attached to them is not what those pages do. Step 8 survives entirely on Roman 2021 p.7-8, which IS a genuine, exactly-quoted precedent (1.3 pp interpreter difference -> 3.2 pp published detectable-change threshold). Bautin is decorative and should be dropped or relabeled. Bautin's own p.1 also carries a caveat the design does not surface but that lands squarely on it: \"these results correspond to a 'best case' scenario... scan-rescan variability may over-simplify the biological reality\" — the exact hazard the design's last failure mode admits (one flight, optimistic sigma_chain).\n(2) SCOPE STRETCH — Liu 2022. The \"intellectual glue\" claim is that L_pdi \"is precisely what makes p(x|y) delivery-invariant, which is exactly the precondition label-shift correction requires.\" Liu p3's precondition is conditional alignment between SOURCE and TARGET domains; in TDI the target domains are 35 unlabeled DATES, while L_pdi supplies alignment only WITHIN two dates across chains. The cited page does not license the inference; the join is the design's own, and the design's own weakest-point section concedes it (F4 has zero paired supervision). Honest disclosure downstream, overclaimed upstream.\n(3) TWO PAGE SLIPS — Liu \"Inference Adjustment\" is cited p5, actually p4 (line 184); Bautin's \"COVs... 0.8% for T1w and 0.57% for T2w\" is cited p5, actually p6 (line 382; the near-identical p5 statement at line 241 reads 0.6%, a source-internal inconsistency, not the design's error). Trivial.\n\nXing is used correctly but note the boundary: Xing p11 supports only that simple averaging is LESS SENSITIVE to which interpreters are chosen, not that it is more ACCURATE — Xing's own conclusion (line 1712) says \"in the absence of gold standard reference data, it was not possible to conclude that one approach was better in terms of accurately reflecting the true area.\" The design quotes the supported half and does not overreach.\n\nNothing in the sources is being made to say something it does not say, apart from the Bautin claim label.",
 "fatal_flaw": "EVERY ARM OF THE KILL CRITERION IS WITHIN-DATE; THE DELIVERABLE IS A CROSS-DATE TREND. The primary transfer test (held-out 2017 delivery T3), the primary attribution test (L_pdi on vs off, measured on T3), and the secondary floor test (F7 blocks C, one flight) are all same-date experiments. The failure mode the design itself names as dominant — F4's within-sensor, across-year radiometric greenness gradient (recall -.109), which EM will silently absorb as an ordinary prior shift and return \"a plausible, confident, wrong q_t\" — lives entirely on the cross-year axis and is invisible to all three arms. TDI can pass every criterion it states and still hand back a smooth, confident, sign-wrong trend, which is worse than F1's visible sawtooth because it survives inspection.\n\nFour subsidiary defects compound it. (a) The reference for the primary test is the model's OWN matched T1/T2 outputs, and T1/T2 are both in the pair bank — the model is scored against itself on its own training deliveries. Worse for the attribution arm: the reference moves between the two conditions, so L_pdi can shrink the T3 gap by pulling T2 toward T1's chain rather than by improving T3. F3's reference-matched T1/T2/T3 fractions are already measured facts and would be a fixed external anchor; the design declines to use them. (b) A degenerate constant-prediction model — the trivial minimizer the design names as \"consistency collapse\" — passes all three arms trivially (T3 gap 0, IoU 1.0). Lambda capping on blocks B is monitoring, not a kill arm; there is no accuracy floor (e.g. 2020 block B F1) conjoined to the criterion. (c) The 1.07 pp bar is the loosest reading of F3: 1.07 pp is the three-way SPREAD under reference-matched cuts, and requiring T3 within 1.07 pp of a two-delivery MEAN is the maximum deviation consistent with that spread, not the comparable quantity. (d) The 0.3 pp attribution margin sits below the 1.3 pp processing-chain noise floor the design itself publishes, at n=1 delivery — it cannot separate signal from the noise it just measured.\n\nREPAIRABLE FROM THE DESIGN'S OWN INPUTS, and that is why this is \"fatal as written,\" not \"fatal in principle\": step 10 already contains the only cross-decade anchor in the design — lidar 2005 vs 2016 — and demotes it to a \"sanity check.\" Promoting it to a kill arm (the TDI 2005->2016 CHANGE must land within a stated band of the lidar change after the height-cut-off level offset, which Roman p7 quantifies at ~4.3%) costs nothing, uses no third lidar epoch, and is the only stated test that can see F4.",
 "best_idea_to_graft": "THE ARCHIVE'S ACCIDENTAL NATURAL EXPERIMENTS AS A ZERO-TEMPORAL-CONFOUND PAIR BANK, WHOSE RESIDUAL IS PUBLISHED AS THE MINIMUM-DETECTABLE-CHANGE FLOOR. Two separable pieces, and the graft should take the second even if it rejects the first.\n\nRobust piece (take unconditionally): sigma_chain measured from same-date multi-delivery residuals (F7's one flight delivered two ways, F3's 2017 triple) is a real, cheap, zero-temporal-confound estimate of processing-chain noise, and attaching it to every published trend number as a detectable-change floor is directly precedented in-domain by Roman 2021 p7-8, whose \"any 40-year UTC changes larger than 3.2 percentage points are considered detectable change\" I verified verbatim. This survives even if L_pdi fails its own attribution test, requires no training, no GPU, and no new labels — it is a reporting discipline any winner can adopt in an afternoon. It also converts F7 from a complaint into an instrument.\n\nTestable-but-uncertain piece (take with the fix): the reframing of F7/F3 as a FOUND overlap cohort — DeepHarmony had to prospectively scan 12 subjects twice, HACA3+ needed a phantom who traveled to 116 sites, and this archive already contains its equivalents by accident. That is genuinely new for this problem and is the one thing that works retrospectively on 20 years that can never be re-flown. Graft it with two amendments: score the transfer against F3's fixed reference-matched fractions rather than the model's own T1/T2 average, and conjoin an accuracy floor plus the lidar 2005->2016 change test so a degenerate or radiometrically-drifting model cannot pass.\n\nAlso worth grafting independently of everything above: step 4a's REFUSAL. The explicit, sourced argument against a program/year domain-confusion head — program is confounded with year, prevalence differs by year, so marginal alignment can suppress the real ~2 pp signal (Liu p2's \"phenomenon of misalignment,\" verified) — is the sharpest negative result in the design and should warn every competing entry away from the domain-adversarial route.",
 "verdict": "GRAFT, DO NOT ADOPT AS-IS. Total 17/18 on the rubric, and the only sub-2 is the one that matters most: the kill criterion. On the source-auditor lens this is the cleanest submission I could ask for — 21/21 quotes verbatim, 19/21 at the exact cited page, pagination honestly declared \"n/a\" where the extraction has none rather than invented, and load-bearing citations (Alexandari's calibrate-then-EM, Liu's inference adjustment, Nguyen's \"forest cover remains mainly unchanged,\" Roman's detectable-change threshold, Xing's SA-vs-LCM) all used for what the pages actually say. The one real audit failure is Bautin: the quotes are genuine but the claim attached to them — \"the move from scan-rescan variability to a minimum detectable atrophy\" — is not what Bautin does; it derives minimum sample size from inter-subject SD, which does not transfer to an n=1 city. Drop Bautin; Roman 2021 already carries step 8 properly and is quoted exactly.\n\nThe design clears the Condition-B checklist on five of six items decisively and honestly. It needs no radiometric cross-calibration (invariance supervision comes only from two deliveries of the same flight; the only per-date adjustment is a scalar prior that never touches pixel values). It needs no third lidar epoch and says truthfully that deleting both lidar files leaves steps 1-9 unchanged — which, ironically, is exactly the property that makes it unfalsifiable on the axis that will kill it. It attacks the operating point centrally rather than orthogonally, and BCTS-then-EM is the right, correctly-sourced answer to F3's near-binary rasters. Its strongest item is not-smoothing/not-inheritance: there is no temporal term anywhere, the 37 maps are produced independently, and the Nguyen contrast is the best-argued paragraph in the submission — Nguyen has to engineer a gradient-magnitude escape hatch to stop its temporal loss eating real forest loss, and TDI needs no escape hatch because it has nothing to escape from. That is the correct structural answer to F5 and F6, and it is quoted accurately.\n\nWhat sinks it as a standalone is that its self-assessment is right and its criterion does not act on it. The author identifies F4 as the fatal blind spot, describes precisely how EM will launder radiometric drift into a plausible prior, notes that the T3 test \"says nothing about cross-year shift\" — and then still ships a kill criterion made entirely of within-year tests, with a self-produced reference, a bar looser than F3's own benchmark, an attribution margin below its own published noise floor, and no guard against the degenerate constant-prediction solution it separately warns about. Honest diagnosis, unenforced. The remedy is already sitting in the design as step 10: promote the lidar 2005->2016 CHANGE comparison from \"external sanity anchor\" to a kill arm. That costs no new data, breaks no Condition-B constraint, and is the only stated test with cross-decade purchase.\n\nRecommended disposition for the winner: take sigma_chain-as-published-MDC-floor unconditionally (it is the cheapest, most robust idea in the whole submission and stands even if the consistency term buys nothing); take the found-overlap-cohort pair bank with the fixed external reference and the lidar-change kill arm bolted on; take step 4a's sourced refusal of the domain-adversarial route as a standing warning to all entries; and leave behind the Bautin sample-size framing and the claim that within-date pairing satisfies Liu's cross-domain precondition."
}

## DESIGN: {
 "name": "PAT — Panel-Anchored Trajectory estimation (fixed paired panel + per-date maps as GREG auxiliaries + measured interpreter detection floor)",
 "angle": "panel-model-assisted — the human paired-point panel is the anchor at every epoch; per-date maps (after reference-matched cuts) are demoted to auxiliary variables inside a model-assisted regression (GREG) estimator; the whole 8-epoch trajectory is fitted with the panel's full T×T covariance matrix rather than as 8 independent years.",
 "one_paragraph": "Stop trying to make 37 heterogeneous deliveries agree with each other, and stop treating the maps as the measurement. Extend the thing F8 already established as the trend authority — the 1,250 fixed paired human-interpreted points — from 2 dates to all 8 map-epochs, interpreted by ONE primary interpreter under a vertex/trajectory protocol in a synchronized multi-date viewer. At each epoch, cut that epoch's probability raster at the threshold that makes its canopy fraction on the panel match the panel's own fraction (the panel, not lidar and not an external target, is the matching reference at every date), then use the resulting map as the auxiliary in McRoberts' model-assisted regression estimator: whole-city map fraction minus the map's bias measured on the panel. Whatever per-date operating-point error, delivery-chain detection bias (F4 ~1 pp) or radiometric greenness offset survives the cut lands in that per-date bias term and is subtracted; the epoch-level estimate stays unbiased regardless of which threshold survived. Because the SAME points are read at every date, epoch estimates are positively covariant, and the trajectory is fitted by GLS using the full 8×8 covariance built from panel residual cross-products — the step Pawlak 2026 skipped (fixed panel, Sen's slope on years treated as independent, every CI covering zero) and the step McRoberts measured to be worth a 1.5–2.0× SE reduction. The detection floor is measured, not assumed: a 10% blind duplicate subsample at every date gives a per-date interpreter discrepancy, Roman's detectable-change threshold is computed per date-pair (Feb–Oct seasons and 5 cm–1 m GSD make one global number wrong), and Hapke's quadrature-over-elapsed-years converts two epoch uncertainties into a rate LoD. The honest structure: the PANEL carries the change signal; the MAPS buy per-date level precision, stratified allocation, and spatial attribution — and one number, computable today on the existing 1,250 points with zero new labelling, says whether the maps buy anything for change at all.",
 "inputs": [
  "ARCHIVE: exactly 8 acquisitions — the same 8 map-epochs already used in the F5 flicker census, one delivery per epoch. The set must include 2005 and 2016 (lidar cross-check epochs), 2017 (F3's three-delivery epoch), 2020 (hand-label epoch) and 2024 (F1 interval endpoint). The other 29 acquisitions are NOT used in the estimator; three are held out as internal consistency tests (Step 13): the two extra 2017 deliveries and the second rendering of the F7 same-flight pair.",
  "MODEL: one frozen segmentation model — the same frozen model that produced F7's 1.3 pp same-flight noise floor. Not retrained, not fine-tuned per date, not domain-adapted. One inference pass per epoch to obtain per-date probabilities, because F3 established that two of three delivered probability rasters were near-binary and unusable as a score.",
  "PANEL (base): the existing 1,250 F8 paired points with their known inclusion weights and their existing 2016 and 2024 labels. No new base sample is drawn; interpretation is extended to the remaining 6 epochs.",
  "PANEL (augmentation, optional, costed separately): ~400 points with known inclusion probabilities in two strata — (a) an edge-buffer stratum within 4 m of a reference-matched crown edge (F6: verified losses median 2.0 m from a crown edge); (b) a map-flagged-change stratum (reference-matched map label differs between adjacent epochs). Chesapeake-style change stratification. The base 1,250 can only be POST-stratified; this augmentation is what actually raises power on dispersed loss.",
  "LABELS: the 2020 hand labels, used only as an external check on the 2020 epoch estimate — never as training data, never inside the estimator.",
  "LIDAR: 2005 and 2016 only, used only to score panel labels at panel points at those two dates. No lidar is needed at the other six epochs because the panel is the matching reference at every date.",
  "INTERPRETERS: one primary (labels the entire panel trajectory) plus one independent second interpreter used ONLY on the duplicate subsample to measure the detection floor.",
  "A written response design document (a JRD analogue) fixing the tree/shrub rule, sub-pixel dominance rule, parallax/misregistration handling and vertex rules — frozen before interpretation begins."
 ],
 "steps": [
  "1. FREEZE THE EPOCH SET. Declare the 8 epochs and the one delivery used per epoch before any interpretation. Record each epoch's GSD, flight month, delivery program and band set — these are needed in Step 11 because interpreter error is not constant across a 5 cm Feb frame and a 1 m Aug frame.",
  "2. ONE FROZEN INFERENCE PASS. Run the single frozen F7 model over all 8 epochs at native GSD, writing per-date probability rasters. No cross-date normalization, no histogram matching, no PIF/IR-MAD, no per-date retraining. ≤2 GPU-days for 8 × 25 km².",
  "3. BUILD THE AUXILIARY VECTOR. For each panel point i and epoch t, extract from that epoch's raster only: p_i(t), the model probability at the point, and f_i(t), the canopy fraction of the reference-matched binary map in a 30 m window centred on the point. f is load-bearing — a continuous 0–1 quantity whose rank across the city is far more stable under a threshold shift than the point's own binary label. Compute the same two quantities city-wide (the whole-city map fraction is the GREG's first term).",
  "4. FIX THE PANEL. Take the 1,250 F8 points with their existing weights. Do NOT redraw. If augmenting, draw the ~400 stratified points now with inclusion probabilities recorded, and carry stratum weights through every subsequent formula.",
  "5. RESPONSE DESIGN AND FIRST-PASS INTERPRETATION. Build a synchronized multi-date viewer showing all 8 epochs of a point simultaneously at matched scale. ONE primary interpreter labels the whole trajectory using a vertex protocol: record canopy/non-canopy at vertices, extrapolate back to the previous vertex, exactly as TimeSync converts vertex attributes into annual labels. A single interpreter is deliberate — a single interpreter's systematic level bias (where they cut large-shrub vs. small-tree) cancels almost entirely in the DIFFERENCE between two dates, which is the quantity of interest; Roman used a single interpreter for precisely this reason. Output: y_i(t) ∈ {0,1} for i = 1..1,250 (or 1,650), t = 1..8.",
  "6. QA/QC — MEASURE, DON'T ASSUME (two separate subsamples). (6a) DUPLICATE subsample: a simple random 10% of panel points (125), independently re-interpreted at all 8 dates by the second interpreter under the same protocol. Because it is a probability subsample, the resulting agreement estimates are themselves design-valid. Output: per-date pairwise agreement a_t and per-date interpreter level difference δ_t = |p_A(t) − p_B(t)|. (6b) BLIND ANTI-SMOOTHING subsample: a DIFFERENT random 10% (125 points) re-interpreted by the PRIMARY interpreter with the 8 dates presented one at a time in shuffled order, no trajectory visible, after a washout. Output: Δ_blind for 2016→2024 on those points, to be compared with Δ_vertex on the same points.",
  "7. PER-DATE REFERENCE-MATCHED CUT. For each epoch t, choose τ_t such that (1/n)Σ_i 1[p_i(t) > τ_t] = (1/n)Σ_i y_i(t). This is a per-date, self-contained calibration — no DN, index or reflectance value is ever compared across dates, and no external reference is needed. It is the operational form of F3's reference-matched cuts (which collapsed the 10.1 pp same-year delivery spread to 1.07 pp) with the panel supplying the reference at every date. Re-derive f_i(t) and the whole-city map fraction at τ_t.",
  "8. PER-EPOCH GREG. μ̂_t = (1/N)Σ_{i=1..N} ŷ_i(t) − (1/n)Σ_{j∈panel} [ŷ_j(t) − y_j(t)] — the initial (map) estimator minus the expectation of its bias estimate. Var(μ̂_t) = [1/(n(n−1))] Σ_j (ε_j(t) − ε̄(t))², ε_j(t) = ŷ_j(t) − y_j(t). Optionally replace binary ŷ with a per-date working model m_t(f, p) fitted on the panel (logistic, FREE intercept and slope); the GREG form and its variance estimator are unchanged, and the free intercept is what makes the estimate insensitive to a per-date level shift in the auxiliary.",
  "9. BUILD THE FULL 8×8 COVARIANCE. For every pair (s,t): Cov(μ̂_s, μ̂_t) = [1/(n(n−1))] Σ_j (ε_j(s) − ε̄(s))(ε_j(t) − ε̄(t)) — McRoberts' Eq. 13 generalized from two dates to eight. This is the entire reason for a FIXED panel: the same points read at every date make these covariances positive, and they subtract from the variance of every difference and of the fitted slope.",
  "10. FIT THE TRAJECTORY BY GLS. Fit canopy fraction vs. year by generalized least squares on (μ̂_1..μ̂_8) with covariance Σ from Step 9. Report slope, SE, 95% CI. Also fit a two-segment piecewise model and compare, because a straight line cannot see trend reversal or acceleration — Hapke's stated caveat for shoreline-rate regression. Report BOTH the GLS slope (headline) and the per-epoch series with error bars, so a reader sees the shape and not only the slope.",
  "11. DERIVE THE DETECTION FLOOR FROM MEASURED INTERPRETER ERROR — PER DATE-PAIR, NOT GLOBALLY. (11a) From Step 6a's δ_t, form Roman's threshold as the sum of the two dates' between-interpreter level discrepancies: DCT(s,t) = δ_s + δ_t. Computed per pair rather than once for the study, because our epochs span 5 cm–1 m GSD and Feb–Oct flights, and Pengra showed interpreter consistency tracks imagery quality. (11b) Per-date total uncertainty U_t = sqrt(SE_samp,t² + SE_interp,t²), Hapke's quadrature uncertainty-budget construction; the interpreter term must be propagated, since failing to account for reference-label variability can underestimate the SE by a factor of 2.3. (11c) Rate LoD(s,t) = sqrt(U_s² + U_t²)/(t − s), Hapke's end-point rate uncertainty. (11d) Required panel size by inverting Bautin's paired-design formula: n = (z_{α/2} + z_β)² (σ_diff/Δ)², with σ_diff the SD of the point-level paired difference y_i(t) − y_i(s) and Δ = 0.02 (the 2 pp target). Report the n it demands.",
  "12. RUN THE KILL TEST — DO THIS FIRST IN PRACTICE, IT NEEDS ZERO NEW LABELS. On the existing 1,250 points at 2016 and 2024, with Step 7's cuts applied to both epochs, compute per point d_i = [ŷ_i(2024) − ŷ_i(2016)] − [y_i(2024) − y_i(2016)] (the map's change error) and c_i = y_i(2024) − y_i(2016) (the panel's observed change). Compute R = Σ(d_i − d̄)² / Σ(c_i − c̄)² — exactly the ratio of the model-assisted change variance to the bare-panel change variance. Report R for BOTH auxiliaries: R_binary (matched binary map) and R_frac (the neighbourhood-fraction working model of Step 8).",
  "13. INTERNAL VALIDATION ON OUR OWN MEASURED FACTS. (13a) F3 test: push all THREE 2017 deliveries independently through Steps 7–8; their three μ̂_2017 values must agree within max(DCT_2017, 2·SE(μ̂_2017)). Raw they spread 10.1 pp, matched-cut 1.07 pp; the GREG bias term should close most of what remains, and if it does not, the auxiliary is delivery-dependent in a way the estimator cannot absorb. (13b) F7 test: push both renderings of the same flight through Steps 7–8; their μ̂ difference must be smaller than the 1.3 pp raw processing-chain floor. (13c) Anti-smoothing test: compare Δ_vertex and Δ_blind on the Step 6b subsample.",
  "14. EXTERNAL CHECKS. Panel labels vs 2020 hand labels at panel points; panel labels vs lidar CHM at panel points for 2005 and 2016 — Roman's 93.4% interpretation-vs-lidar agreement gives both the expected magnitude and the expected DIRECTION of disagreement (interpreters call large-shrub/small-tree as canopy where a height cut-off does not).",
  "15. REPORT. GLS slope ± CI as headline; per-epoch series with U_t; per-pair DCT and rate LoD tables; R; the three internal-validation numbers; and an explicit statement of which epoch pairs the estimator can and cannot separate."
 ],
 "outputs": "A GLS-fitted 20-year canopy trajectory: 8 error-adjusted epoch estimates μ̂_t with per-epoch total uncertainty U_t (sampling ⊕ interpreter, in quadrature), the full 8×8 covariance matrix Σ, and a slope in pp/yr with a 95% CI that has the panel covariance built in — plus a two-segment fit for shape. Alongside it: a measured per-date-pair detectable-change threshold table DCT(s,t) and a rate-LoD table, so any claimed change reads as detectable or not; the required-n figure from the paired power formula; the number R that says whether the maps helped the change estimate at all; and three internal-consistency numbers (three 2017 deliveries reconciled, F7 same-flight pair reconciled, vertex-vs-blind interpretation gap). Secondary and explicitly non-authoritative: the reference-matched per-epoch rasters, retained unmodified, used only for stratification and for showing WHERE the panel's change points fall — never for the citywide number.",
 "checklist": {
  "no_radiometric_calibration": "HOLDS, structurally. No DN, index, or reflectance value is ever compared across dates. Each epoch's map is cut at a threshold set on that epoch's OWN panel labels (Step 7), which is the same logic Cai et al. used for Orlando: 'This year-by-year threshold adjustment ensures that the classification process is entirely independent for each time period' (cai2025, p.6). The response variable — the human trajectory label — is radiometry-free by construction. Whatever the F4 whole-distribution radiometric greenness shift does to a given epoch's map, it produces a per-date level offset in the auxiliary, which the GREG bias term subtracts (Step 8) and which the working model's free intercept absorbs. Nothing in the pipeline requires the archive to be radiometrically comparable; it requires only that each epoch's map be internally rank-informative within itself.",
  "no_extra_lidar": "HOLDS. Lidar 2005 and 2016 are used ONLY to score panel labels at panel points at those two dates (Step 14), following Roman's precedent of checking a 2010 interpretation against 2008 lidar. The six other epochs need no lidar because the panel itself is the matching reference at every date (Step 7). No lidar is used for training, for thresholding, or in the estimator. If the two lidar epochs vanished tomorrow the design would still run, losing only one external check.",
  "operating_point": "ADDRESSED IN TWO LAYERS, one empirical and one structural. (a) Empirical: Step 7 applies exactly the reference-matched cut that F3 measured — three same-year 2017 deliveries spread 10.1 pp at delivered thresholds and 1.07 pp under reference-matched cuts. (b) Structural: whatever bias survives the cut is subtracted by the GREG's per-date bias term, McRoberts' 'difference between the initial estimator and the expectation of its bias estimate' (r2_29, p.151). Concretely, the F4 ~1 pp residual same-year delivery-chain detection bias BECOMES the per-date GREG intercept, estimated from the panel at that date, not carried into the trend. MY OWN DERIVATION, NOT A CITATION: a GREG whose working model has a per-date free intercept and slope is exactly invariant to an affine distortion of the auxiliary — the intercept eats the offset, the slope eats the gain, and the bias term is re-estimated on that date's own labels. The invariance is EXACT only for affine distortion; a threshold shift on a probability raster is monotone but not affine in the 30 m neighbourhood fraction, so it is approximate, and F3's residual 1.07 pp spread under matched cuts is the empirical bound on how far from exact. Step 13a tests this directly on the three 2017 deliveries.",
  "not_smoothing_not_inheritance": "HOLDS on the algorithm side, MEASURED (not assumed) on the human side. No map pixel value is ever modified; no temporal filter, median, HMM or trajectory-fit is applied to any map; no epoch's value is copied forward or inherited from a neighbouring epoch. Each epoch estimate is anchored on that epoch's own independent panel labels, so a dispersed loss enters proportionally through a probability sample rather than having to survive a stability prior — and the optional edge-buffer stratum (F6: verified losses median 2.0 m from a crown edge) oversamples exactly where dispersed loss lives, with known inclusion probability, following the Chesapeake change-AA practice of stratifying on mapped change and then checking change omission by distance to change patch. The ONE real smoothing risk is human: a vertex protocol shows the interpreter all 8 dates at once and can under-call a single-date dispersed loss the same way a smoother would. That risk is not waved away — Step 6b measures it directly against a blind, shuffled, per-date re-interpretation and it carries its own kill number (see expected_failure_modes and kill_criterion).",
  "scales": "HOLDS. GPU: one frozen model, one inference pass, 8 epochs × 25 km² — ≤2 GPU-days including re-tiling, and zero training. HUMAN, with arithmetic: 1,250 points × 8 epochs = 10,000 point-dates, but the vertex protocol means an interpreter makes ONE decision covering all 8 dates at a stable point, since vertex attributes are 'converted into annual labels by extrapolating the recorded values at each vertex back until another labeled vertex is reached' (pengra, p.5). At ~5 s per stable trajectory glance plus ~20 s per vertex decision, first pass ≈ 20–25 h. Duplicate subsample (125 pts × 8, second interpreter, same protocol) ≈ 3 h. Blind shuffled subsample (125 pts × 8 = 1,000 full-cost independent decisions) ≈ 6 h. Total ≈ 30–34 h ≈ 4–5 person-days, plus ~1 day for the optional 400-point stratified augmentation and ~1 day for estimation and reporting: 5–7 human-days base, 8–10 with augmentation. No bespoke sensors, no new flights, no new hand-label campaign.",
  "kill_criterion": "R ≥ 1.0. Computable TODAY on the existing 1,250 paired points at 2016 and 2024 with zero new interpretation (Step 12). R = Σ(d_i − d̄)² / Σ(c_i − c̄)² is the ratio of the model-assisted change variance to the bare-panel change variance. If R ≥ 1.0 for BOTH the binary and the neighbourhood-fraction auxiliary, the maps make the change estimate LESS precise than a bare panel and the 'model-assisted' half of this design is dead — what remains is Roman 2021 with more epochs and a covariance matrix, which is defensible but is not this design. Secondary kill (response design): |Δ_vertex − Δ_blind| > 1.0 pp over 2016→2024 on the Step 6b subsample — half the 8-year signal — means the trajectory protocol is itself smoothing, which is precisely what Condition B forbids, and the response design must be replaced with fully blind per-date interpretation at roughly 4× the human cost. Third kill (affordability): if Step 11d's required n exceeds 3,500 points to reach SE(Δ) ≤ 0.51 pp (the SE needed for a 2 pp / 8 yr signal to clear zero at 95%), the design cannot be delivered inside a few human-days and should be abandoned rather than under-powered."
 },
 "expected_failure_modes": [
  "R ≥ 1 — the most likely single outcome, and I expect it for the binary auxiliary. F5 (50.7% of ever-canopy pixels flicker across 8 maps) and F7 (1.3 pp same-flight processing floor) both say the map's change signal is noisier than the truth's change signal at a point. When map errors anti-correlate across dates (flicker), Cov(μ̂_s, μ̂_t) in Step 9 goes NEGATIVE and inflates rather than deflates the difference variance. The design degrades gracefully — the GREG is still unbiased, you just lose the precision gain — but the novelty claim collapses to 'a well-instrumented panel survey'.",
  "Interpreter smoothing under the vertex protocol. Showing all 8 dates at once and asking for vertices biases toward under-calling single-date dispersed loss — the F6 signal. Xing's evidence is the alarm: 'Forest gain had the lowest agreement, with only one pixel labeled as forest gain by a majority of interpreters. Forest loss had only two sample pixels (10%), for which all interpreters agreed, and a majority of the seven interpreters assigned forest loss to only 40% of all pixels for which at least one interpreter labeled the pixel as forest loss' (r2_25, p.7). Interpreters disagree MOST on exactly the transitions that carry our signal. Step 6b measures the gap; it cannot eliminate it inside budget.",
  "Per-date DCT heterogeneity swamping the GLS slope. Epochs range 5 cm to 1 m GSD and Feb to Oct flight; the 1 m leaf-off epochs will have visibly worse interpreter agreement, so U_t is strongly heteroscedastic. If the GLS weights are right the coarse epochs are correctly downweighted, but if σ_interp is misestimated on a small 125-point duplicate subsample, one bad epoch can drive the slope. Mitigation: report the slope with and without each epoch (leave-one-epoch-out).",
  "Definition drift in the RESPONSE, not the map. At 1 m a large shrub reads as canopy; at 5 cm it does not. The per-date GREG intercept absorbs this for the AUXILIARY but cannot absorb it for the PANEL, because the panel is the estimand's definition. This is a genuine residual bias correlated with GSD, and since GSD is correlated with year, it is correlated with the trend. The written response design and duplicate QA reduce it; nothing in this design removes it.",
  "Edge-buffer oversampling makes measured agreement look worse. F6 puts real change a median 2.0 m from a crown edge, which is exactly where parallax, misregistration and interpreter ambiguity all live. Oversampling that stratum raises the fraction of hard points, so a_t drops and DCT widens. That is the correct answer arithmetically and an uncomfortable one to present next to a city's existing headline numbers.",
  "Underestimating the SE by ignoring reference-label variability. Pengra reports that 'failing to account for interpreter variability could produce underestimates of the standard error by a factor of 2.3' (r2_24, p.2). Step 11b propagates it, but a 125-point duplicate subsample estimates σ_interp poorly for rare transitions; the interpreter variance on CHANGE (as opposed to state) may be badly under-resolved.",
  "Cannot detect SHARED interpreter bias. With one primary and one duplicate interpreter, a latent class model is not available — 'it is recommended to apply LCM for three or more manifest variables (i.e., three or more interpreters)' (r2_25, p.6). We are stuck in the simple-averaging worldview, which assumes disagreements are compensating random errors. If both interpreters share a systematic rule (e.g. both consistently call shadowed crown edges non-canopy), the panel's own bias is invisible and the whole trajectory inherits it.",
  "The linear summary hides a real reversal. Hapke's caveat applies verbatim: 'Linear regression fails to recognize the potential for temporal differences in trend (trend reversals) and accelerations or decelerations' (shoreline_ofr2010_1118, report p.13). Step 10's piecewise fit is a partial answer, but 8 epochs over 20 years cannot resolve much shape.",
  "Step 13a fails. If the three 2017 deliveries do not reconcile to within DCT_2017 after Steps 7–8, the GREG bias term is not absorbing delivery-chain effects — meaning the auxiliary's error structure differs between deliveries at the POINT level, not just the level. That would invalidate the operating-point argument and force the auxiliary back to a geometry-only construction."
 ],
 "sources": [
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\r2_29_mcroberts2014.txt",
   "page": "p. 151 (PDF p. 3 of 8)",
   "quote": "One form of the model-assisted regression estimator (Särndal, Swensson, & Wretman, 1992, Section 6.5) for μk is defined as the difference between the initial estimator and the expectation of its bias estimate",
   "claim": "Step 8's estimator form: whole-city map fraction minus the map's bias measured on the panel. This is what makes the per-date operating point orthogonal to the epoch estimate — whatever cut survived, its bias is measured on that date's own panel labels and subtracted."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\r2_29_mcroberts2014.txt",
   "page": "p. 151 (PDF p. 3 of 8)",
   "quote": "However, systematic classification errors induce bias into this estimator which, for equal probability samples, can be estimated as, Bias(μ̂k_initial) = (1/n) Σ (ŷik − yki)",
   "claim": "The bias term is estimated per date from the panel — the mechanism by which F4's ~1 pp residual delivery-chain detection bias and the radiometric greenness offset are removed without any cross-date radiometric calibration."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\r2_29_mcroberts2014.txt",
   "page": "p. 155 (PDF p. 7 of 8)",
   "quote": "for both approaches to inference, covariance estimates were positive with the beneficial effect of reducing Var(Δμ̂) as calculated using Eq. (3). These results can be attributed to measurement of the same set of plots in both 2002 and 2007. If the covariances in Eq. (3) were set to zero, as would be expected and justified for measurements of different sets of plots in 2002 and 2007, the model-assisted standard error estimates would have been increased by a factor of approximately 1.5 and the model-based standard error estimates would have been increased by a factor of approximately 2.0.",
   "claim": "The core payoff of a FIXED panel, and the quantified size of it (1.5–2.0× SE reduction). This is why Step 9 builds the full 8×8 covariance instead of treating epochs as independent, and it is the specific correction to Pawlak 2026."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\r2_29_mcroberts2014.txt",
   "page": "p. 150 (PDF p. 2 of 8)",
   "quote": "Under the assumption that classification errors for 2002 and 2007 are independent for different population units, Cov(μ̂2007, μ̂2002) is calculated using the sample data as, [1/(n(n−1))] Σ (εi2007 − ε̄2007)(εi2002 − ε̄2002)",
   "claim": "The exact estimator generalized in Step 9 from a 2×2 to an 8×8 covariance matrix using panel residual cross-products."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\r2_29_mcroberts2014.txt",
   "page": "p. 149 (PDF p. 1 of 8)",
   "quote": "With trajectory analyses, an extensive time series of imagery is typically required (Kennedy, Cohen, & Schroeder, 2007; Zhu, Woodcock, & Olofsson, 2012). With direct classification, large numbers of change observations may be necessary for training the classifier and/or assessing accuracy, a difficult task when rates of change are small and change observations are acquired using equal probability sampling designs. The advantage of post-classification is that the data requirements are much less severe.",
   "claim": "Justifies choosing a post-classification, panel-anchored route over LandTrendr/CCDC-style trajectory algorithms or a direct change classifier, given 8 uncalibrated epochs and a ~2 pp / 8 yr signal."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\roman2021_land_phila.txt",
   "page": "p. 7 of 31",
   "quote": "When we compared a random 5% subsample of points to a second, independent interpreter, we found 91.5% agreement regarding whether a given point was a tree across all years, with better agreement in 2000 and 2010 imagery, 92.9% and 94.9%, respectively, close to other studies of recent imagery [17,76]. The two interpreters' UTC estimates were close: the average difference across all years was 1.3 percentage points.",
   "claim": "The detection floor must be MEASURED from a duplicate subsample, and interpreter agreement varies by epoch imagery quality (92.9%/94.9% on the recent, orthorectified years vs 91.5% overall) — which is why Step 11a computes DCT per date-pair rather than once."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\roman2021_land_phila.txt",
   "page": "p. 8 of 31",
   "quote": "Following eqn. 1, any 40-year UTC changes larger than 3.2 percentage points are considered detectable change.",
   "claim": "The detectable-change-threshold construction adopted in Step 11a: the threshold is the sum of the two dates' between-interpreter discrepancies, computed from measured duplicates and stated as a number before any change is claimed."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\roman2021_land_phila.txt",
   "page": "p. 5 of 31",
   "quote": "When using aerial interpretation of randomly distributed points, potential shortcomings include variability among interpreters and a relatively small number of points [21]. The former issue can be minimized by having a single interpreter [24], which was our approach.",
   "claim": "Step 5's choice of ONE primary interpreter for the whole trajectory. A single interpreter's systematic level bias largely cancels in the between-date difference; the second interpreter exists only to measure the floor, not to produce labels used in the estimate."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\roman2021_land_phila.txt",
   "page": "p. 7 of 31",
   "quote": "Comparing our 2010 interpretation to 2008 LiDAR [74], we found 93.4% agreement regarding whether a given point was a tree or not. Most of the disagreement (4.3%) was due to the interpreter classifying points as tree/shrub that were not categorized as tree by LiDAR.",
   "claim": "Step 14's lidar cross-check at the 2005/2016 epochs, including the expected DIRECTION of disagreement (interpreters over-call relative to a height cut-off) — so a lidar/panel gap is not misread as panel error."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\roman2021_land_phila.txt",
   "page": "p. 8 of 31",
   "quote": "Across the entire city, UTC change was both statistically significant and beyond our detectable change threshold, increasing by 4.3 percentage points (Table 3). Of all points classified as tree citywide in 1970, 65.0% remained stable through 2010",
   "claim": "Point-level canopy is highly persistent (65.0% stable over 40 years), which is the physical reason the panel's epoch estimates are strongly positively covariant and the difference variance collapses — the mechanism Step 9 exploits."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\roman2021_land_phila.txt",
   "page": "p. 6 of 31",
   "quote": "The interpreter followed Nowak and Greenfield [17] to account for parallax and misregistration error. Permanent water (defined as points classified as water in all five images) was excluded... Points which were uninterpretable in any year were also discarded from further analysis.",
   "claim": "The response-design elements that must be written down before Step 5 (parallax/misregistration handling, uninterpretable-point rule) — the same controls F8 already applies for duplicates/blur/drift/capture."
  },

   "page": "p. 4 of 10",
   "quote": "For Sets 1–12, a minimum of 10% of the reference sample pixels were selected for duplicate interpretation via simple random sampling.    "claim": "Step 6a's design: a 10% SIMPLE RANDOM duplicate subsample, so the resulting interpreter-error estimate is itself design-valid and can legitimately feed the detection floor. Purposive duplicates may be added for feedback but must not enter the estimate."

  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\r2_24_pengra.txt",
   "page": "p. 5 of 10",
   "quote": "These attributes are converted into annual labels by extrapolating the recorded values at each vertex back until another labeled vertex is reached.",
   "claim": "The vertex protocol of Step 5 — and the entire human-cost model. It is what turns 10,000 point-dates into roughly 3,000 interpreter decisions and makes 4–5 person-days feasible. It is also the source of the smoothing risk that Step 6b measures."

  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\r2_24_pengra.txt",
   "page": "p. 2 of 10",
   "quote": "McRoberts et al. (2018) quantified the impact of variability in the reference class labels on the standard error of area estimates and found that failing to account for interpreter variability could produce underestimates of the standard error by a factor of 2.3.",
   "claim": "Step 11b must propagate the duplicate-derived interpreter variance into U_t and into the GLS trend SE. Reporting interpreter agreement as a footnote instead of a variance component understates the SE by up to 2.3×."

  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\r2_24_pengra.txt",
   "page": "p. 1 of 10 (abstract)",
   "quote": "Increasing availability of Landsat and Google Earth fine resolution data over the 1984 to 2016 monitoring period coincided with increased interpreter consistency for the post-2000 data record.",
   "claim": "Interpreter consistency tracks imagery quality across a long archive — so a single global detectable-change threshold is wrong for an archive spanning 5 cm–1 m GSD and Feb–Oct flights. DCT must be per date-pair (Step 11a) and U_t heteroscedastic in the GLS (Step 10)."

  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\r2_24_pengra.txt",
   "page": "p. 6 of 10",

   "quote": "Lower agreement between interpreters was observed for land cover change (Table 2).   {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\r2_25_xing.txt",
   "page": "p. 6 of 17",
   "quote": "Although it is recommended to apply LCM for three or more manifest variables (i.e., three or more interpreters), our initial analyses indicated that the LCM estimates using just two interpreters were generally consistent with SA estimates.",
   "claim": "With one primary plus one duplicate interpreter, latent class modelling is not available, so the design operates under the simple-averaging assumption that disagreements are compensating random errors — and therefore cannot detect SHARED interpreter bias.   {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\r2_25_xing.txt",
   "page": "p. 5 of 17",
   "quote": "For SA, inconsistency among interpreters is assumed to be the result of random mis-labeling errors that compensate over the full sample.",
   "claim": "The exact assumption the panel rests on, stated explicitly so a reviewer can attack it. If shared interpreter bias exists, the whole trajectory inherits it and no amount of map auxiliary detects it."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\pawlak2026_california_utf.txt",
   "page": "p. 2 of 53 (abstract)",
   "quote": "Statewide canopy cover showed a modest negative trend from 2016 to 2022 (Sen's slope: -0.60% per year), but confidence intervals included zero across all groups and climate zones, indicating that trends were not statistically distinguishable from no change.",
   "claim": "The specific failure this design corrects. Pawlak had a fixed panel and error-adjusted per-year estimates, and still could not resolve a trend, because the years were treated as independent when the slope was formed."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\pawlak2026_california_utf.txt",
   "page": "p. 12 of 53",
   "quote": "Each point was validated once per year against NAIP imagery (4,800 unique validations were completed in this process).",
   "claim": "Pawlak DID have a fixed panel interpreted on every date — 1,200 urban points × 4 years. So the delta of this design over Pawlak is a correction (use the between-epoch covariance), not the invention of the panel. Stated honestly in novelty."

  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\pawlak2026_california_utf.txt",
   "page": "p. 14 of 53",
   "quote": "To assess change over the broader time series, we calculated Sen's slope using the median of all pairwise slopes between years.    "claim": "The precise step this design replaces: a Sen's slope over per-year point estimates discards the panel covariance entirely. Step 10 substitutes GLS on the 8×8 covariance from Step 9, which McRoberts p.155 measures as worth 1.5–2.0× in SE."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\pawlak2026_california_utf.txt",
   "page": "p. 11 of 53",
   "quote": "Ambiguous pixels were labeled as NA and excluded from the analysis... Unclear points were checked with Google Street View where available and classified as NA if uncertainty remained.    "claim": "Independent confirmation that the ambiguous panel points cluster at crown edges and in shadow — exactly where F6 puts the real change signal. The NA rule must be written into the response design and the NA rate reported per epoch, because dropping NAs at edges silently biases against dispersed edge loss."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\shoreline_ofr2010_1118.txt",
   "page": "report p. 16 (PDF p. 24 of 66)",
   "quote": "The uncertainty of a single transect's end-point shoreline change rate is found as the quadrature addition of the uncertainties for each year's shoreline position, divided by the number of years between the shoreline surveys",
   "claim": "Step 11c's rate limit of detection: LoD_rate(s,t) = sqrt(U_s² + U_t²)/(t − s). Converts two per-epoch uncertainties into a per-year detectability floor that can be compared directly against the ~2 pp / 8 yr = 0.25 pp/yr target."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\shoreline_ofr2010_1118.txt",
   "page": "report p. 12 (PDF p. 20 of 66)",
   "quote": "For each HWL shoreline position, the total uncertainty is found as the square root of the sum of squares (Taylor, 1997) of the relevant uncertainty terms, based on an assumption that each term is random and independent of the others.",
   "claim": "Step 11b's per-epoch uncertainty budget: sampling SE and interpreter SE combined in quadrature, with the independence assumption stated rather than hidden. Also the template for enumerating epoch-specific terms (GSD, season, delivery program) rather than assuming one number for the archive."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\shoreline_ofr2010_1118.txt",
   "page": "report p. 13 (PDF p. 21 of 66)",
   "quote": "Linear regression fails to recognize the potential for temporal differences in trend (trend reversals) and accelerations or decelerations (Fenster and others, 1993; Fenster and Dolan, 1994; Morton, 1991; Morton, 1996), so average trends and rates of shoreline change in this study were calculated for both long-term (entire period) and short-term (most recent) time scales",
   "claim": "Step 10 must report a piecewise/short-window fit alongside the single GLS slope. A single 20-year slope over 8 epochs can hide a reversal, which for a city defending a canopy target is the failure that matters most."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\shoreline_ofr2010_1118.txt",
   "page": "report p. 12 (PDF p. 20 of 66)",
   "quote": "A potentially large source of shoreline position uncertainty that we do not quantify results from the local short-term variability of true shoreline positions... Because of the lack of accurate, systematic data regarding the seasonal variation of the shoreline... the uncertainty values reported here (tables 6a and 6b) do not include an uncertainty term for the seasonal shoreline position variability",
   "claim": "Precedent for explicitly naming an unquantified term in the budget rather than omitting it. Our analogue: the GSD-correlated shrub/tree definition drift in the PANEL, which the GREG intercept cannot absorb and which Step 15 must declare as an unquantified term."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\r2_28_bautin_mdc.txt",
   "page": "p. 4 of 10",
   "quote": "The minimum sample size necessary to detect an atrophy in a longitudinal (within subject repeated measures) study was computed based on a two-sample bilateral paired t-test using the following formula (Altmann et al., 2009): npaired = (zα/2 + zβ)² (σdiff / Δgroup)² / 2 where σdiff is the standard deviation of the difference",
   "claim": "Step 11d's required-panel-size calculation and the third kill criterion. Because the panel is PAIRED, the relevant variance is σ of the point-level difference, not σ of the level — which is why 1,250 paired points can resolve a signal that 1,250 independent points per date could not."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\r2_28_bautin_mdc.txt",
   "page": "p. 4 of 10",
   "quote": "These metrics aim at representing a scan-rescan variability, although without the additional \"real-life\" factors contributing to scan-rescan variance such as different shimming parameters, scanner drifts and motion patterns.",
   "claim": "Precedent for the caution that a controlled repeatability experiment underestimates operational variance. Our Step 6a duplicate subsample measures interpreter-vs-interpreter variance under identical conditions and will therefore UNDERSTATE the real per-date uncertainty; Step 15 must say so."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\cai2025_orlando_biennial.txt",
   "page": "p. 6 of 14",
   "quote": "This year-by-year threshold adjustment ensures that the classification process is entirely independent for each time period and, importantly, independent from the validation samples themselves, effectively mitigating the risk of accuracy overestimation.",
   "claim": "Precedent for per-date thresholds set independently at every epoch (Step 7) instead of one archive-wide recipe — and the warning attached to it: our cut is set ON the panel, so the panel points used for the cut are not independent of the estimate. Step 8's GREG bias term is computed on the SAME panel, which is legitimate for the GREG (the bias is what is being estimated) but means the accuracy figures cannot be quoted as independent validation."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\cai2025_orlando_biennial.txt",
   "page": "p. 5 of 14",
   "quote": "Classification results are determined by distinct, year-specific thresholds, meticulously adjusted to account for inherent inconsistencies in the imagery's spectral and radiometric responses.",
   "claim": "Direct precedent that per-year threshold adjustment is the accepted operational substitute for radiometric cross-calibration on an uncalibrated multi-year aerial archive."
  },
  {
   "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\chesapeake_change_accuracy_methods.txt",
   "page": "slide 12 of 20",
   "quote": "Locating change omission. Assess validity of stratification by distance to change patch of same T1 LC value for no change samples",
   "claim": "Step 4's augmentation and Step 13's omission check: when you stratify on mapped change you must actively test for change the map MISSED, by measuring distance from no-change samples to the nearest change patch of the same T1 class.    "file": "C:\\Users\\Kameron\\AppData\\Local\\Temp\\claude\\C--Users-Kameron\\43e2ce58-528b-45a8-b9d9-c8db345691a7\\scratchpad\\txt\\chesapeake_change_accuracy_methods.txt",
   "page": "slide 10 of 20",
   "quote": "Possible class strata options ○ 144 possible change classes ○ Ignoring low-frequency change classes ■ Ex. - 16 classes account for approximately 95% of mapped change in PG County, 56 classes make up the remaining 5% ... ● Proportional sample distribution + square root transformation",

   "quote": "Create 3x3 pixel window, throw out sample if surrounding 8 pixels include another land change value (no change is acceptable) ● Throw out samples directly adjacent to other samples unless no other possible location",

 ],
 "novelty_vs_known_families": "Against TEMPORAL FILTERING / HMM (Abercrombie & Friedl, Taff's prior reweighting, the smoothing pilot that moved F5's estimate away from truth): PAT applies no filter of any kind. No map pixel value is modified, no state is carried across epochs, no prior is imposed on the trajectory. The trajectory is fitted in the ESTIMATE space (8 scalars plus a covariance matrix), never in the map space — so a dispersed loss cannot be filtered out of existence, because it was never inside a filter. Against CALIBRATED TIME-SERIES ALGORITHMS (CCDC/LCMAP, LandTrendr, Zhu 2017): those require a dense, radiometrically consistent, single-sensor stack. We have 8 epochs, 5 delivery programs and no calibration. McRoberts states the tradeoff plainly (p.149): trajectory analyses need an extensive time series, direct change classification needs many change observations, and post-classification is the route when neither exists. PAT is the post-classification route with the panel supplying inference. Against TRAJECTORY / TIMESYNC RESPONSE DESIGNS: we borrow LCMAP's vertex protocol wholesale as a COST device (Pengra p.5) and borrow its 10% random-duplicate QA wholesale as a MEASUREMENT device (p.4). What is new is the use: LCMAP uses TimeSync to validate a calibrated Landsat map series; here the interpretations are the estimand's anchor and the uncalibrated multi-vendor aerial maps are the auxiliary, i.e. the roles are inverted. We also add something LCMAP does not have — a blind, shuffled, per-date re-interpretation subsample whose only job is to measure whether the trajectory protocol itself is smoothing. Against METHOD-HOLDING / HARMONIZATION (Toronto, Portland, CMM frozen recipes; DeepHarmony, HACA3, CALAMITI, Mboga's DANN/D-CORAL, IR-MAD/PIF, histogram matching): PAT is the deliberate opposite. It does not harmonize the archive, does not learn a shared feature space, does not normalize radiometry, and does not freeze a recipe. It lets every epoch be as inconsistent as it is and absorbs that inconsistency in a per-date bias term estimated from that date's own human labels. F2 already established that post-hoc harmonization cannot work here (<0.1 pp effect); PAT's answer is to stop harmonizing rather than to harmonize harder. Against SAMPLE-BASED CHANGE ESTIMATION (Roman 2021, Tampa dot grid, Nowak & Greenfield, Olofsson 2013/2014, Pawlak 2026) — the nearest family, and the one where I must be most careful. Roman 2021 is a fixed panel with a measured detectable-change threshold; Pawlak 2026 is a fixed panel with error-adjusted per-year estimates. Neither is novel to invent. Four things are genuinely added. (1) The maps enter as GREG auxiliaries rather than being discarded (Roman) or being the estimand (Pawlak/Olofsson), so the per-date estimate gets whatever precision the map can honestly supply while the panel keeps the anchor. (2) The per-date free intercept + slope is what makes the estimator insensitive to the operating point — my derivation from the GREG form, exact for affine distortion, approximate for a threshold shift, with F3's residual 1.07 pp as the empirical bound. (3) The full 8×8 panel covariance and a GLS trend, generalizing McRoberts' two-date Eq. 13 to T dates. This is the concrete correction to Pawlak, who HAD the panel (4,800 validations, one per point per year) and then took a Sen's slope over years treated as independent, and got CIs covering zero everywhere. McRoberts p.155 prices exactly that omission at 1.5–2.0× the SE. (4) The detection floor is per date-pair rather than global, because our archive spans 5 cm–1 m and Feb–Oct and Pengra shows agreement tracks imagery quality — Roman's single 3.2 pp number would be wrong for us in both directions. Against McRoberts 2014 specifically: he had two dates, one calibrated sensor, and an auxiliary whose classification accuracy was ~0.9. We have eight dates, five delivery programs, no calibration, and an auxiliary that F1 shows is SIGN-WRONG for change. The extension is the T-date covariance plus an explicit, pre-registered test (R) of whether such an auxiliary is admissible at all — a question McRoberts never had to ask.",
 "cost_estimate": "GPU: ≤2 GPU-days total. One frozen model (the F7 model), one inference pass, 8 epochs × 25 km². No training, no fine-tuning, no domain adaptation, no per-date model. If the delivered probability rasters turn out usable as scores for a majority of epochs, this drops toward 0.5 GPU-day. HUMAN (arithmetic, not a guess): 1,250 points × 8 epochs = 10,000 point-dates, but the vertex protocol collapses a stable point to one decision covering all 8 dates. At ~5 s per stable-trajectory glance plus ~20 s per vertex decision, and assuming roughly 10–15% of points carry at least one vertex, first pass ≈ 20–25 h. Duplicate subsample (125 pts × 8, second interpreter) ≈ 3 h. Blind shuffled subsample (125 pts × 8 = 1,000 independent full-cost decisions at ~20 s) ≈ 6 h. Subtotal ≈ 30–34 h ≈ 4–5 person-days. Add ~1 person-day for the optional 400-point stratified augmentation (drawing, protocol, interpretation) and ~1 person-day for estimation, covariance assembly, GLS, the three internal-validation runs and reporting. TOTAL: 5–7 human-days base, 8–10 with augmentation, ≤2 GPU-days. Front-loaded and cheap: the kill test (Step 12) runs on the existing 1,250 points at 2016/2024 with ZERO new interpretation and roughly 2 GPU-hours of inference, so the go/no-go decision costs under a day before any of the human budget is committed. No new flights, no new sensors, no new hand-label campaign, no lidar beyond the two epochs already held.",
 "self_assessed_weakest_point": "Two, and the first is structural. (1) The design's NOVELTY rests on R < 1 — that the reference-matched maps make the change estimate more precise than a bare panel — and my own reading of our measured facts says R < 1 is probably FALSE, at least for the binary auxiliary. F5 (50.7% of ever-canopy pixels flicker across 8 maps) and F7 (1.3 pp same-flight processing floor) both mean the map's point-level change error is likely to be larger and, worse, anti-correlated across dates, which drives the Step 9 covariance negative and inflates rather than deflates the difference variance. If that happens, what I have built is a well-instrumented, honestly-floored panel survey — Roman 2021 with eight epochs and a proper covariance matrix, plus per-date GREG level corrections that still help the epoch estimates. That is a defensible and probably correct answer for this city, but the \"model-assisted\" framing would be decoration on the part that matters, the trend. I have made this testable rather than hidden it, and the test costs under a day, but I cannot claim the design's headline before running it. (2) The vertex/trajectory response design is the only thing that makes the human cost fit inside \"a few human-days,\" and it is a TEMPORAL SMOOTHER relocated from the algorithm into the interpreter. A human shown all eight dates at once and asked where the vertices are will under-call a single-epoch dispersed loss for the same reason a median filter does — and F6 says our real signal is dispersed, edge-concentrated, with the largest persistent loss cluster only 0.41 ha. Xing's numbers are the warning shot: a majority of seven trained interpreters assigned forest loss to only 40% of the pixels any of them called forest loss, and forest gain had a majority on exactly one pixel. I measure the smoothing with the blind shuffled subsample and I give it a kill number (1.0 pp), but if it fails, the fix — fully blind per-date interpretation of 10,000 point-dates — costs roughly 4× the human budget and blows the Condition-B scaling requirement. So the design is, at its cost floor, betting that a human trajectory reader is meaningfully less biased than a temporal filter, and I have evidence that they disagree most on precisely the transitions I need. Third, smaller but real: the GSD-correlated shrub/tree definition drift lives in the PANEL, not the map, so the GREG intercept cannot absorb it, and because GSD is correlated with year it is correlated with the trend. I can only declare it as an unquantified term in the budget, the way Hapke declares seasonal shoreline variability."
}

### JUDGMENT: {
 "design_name": "PAT — Panel-Anchored Trajectory estimation (fixed paired panel + per-date maps as GREG auxiliaries + measured interpreter detection floor)",
 "scores": {
  "no_radiometric_calibration": 2,
  "no_extra_lidar": 2,
  "operating_point": 2,
  "not_smoothing_not_inheritance": 1,
  "scales": 1,
  "kill_criterion": 2,
